The key benefit of the additive model lies in simplifying the complexity of the underlying function class. In other words, the collection of additive functions defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is considerably “smaller” than the set encompassing all functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This simplicity of additive models is reflected in their rate of estimation.
[
137
]
proved that for an additive regression model, if each univariate component
f_{j}
is
\beta
-Hölder, then the minimax optimal rate for estimating
f
is
C_{d}n^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}
(for fixed dimension, which is absorbed in the constant). This minimax rate reveals that the additive model can evade the curse of dimensionality to a large extent as
d
does not appear in the exponent of
n
.
Along with its various generalizations, this model inspires a line of research that encompasses but is not limited to
[
138
,
73
,
26
,
49
,
75
]
. Recently, efforts have been to made to under the estimation error of additive model and its generalization via deep neural networks in several paper (e.g., see
[
7
]
,
[
86
]
,
[
87
]
,
[
131
]
and reference therein).

The case of
d
growing with
n
is much less understood. Now there is a need to track the precise effect of
d
in the constant
C_{d}
. When
d/n\to 0
but
d
slowly increases with
n
,
[
128
]
established that the minimax optimal rate of estimation of the additive mean function
f_{\star}
is
dn^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}
(where each component is
\beta
-smooth) when the coordinates of
X
are independent, i.e. the optimal factor for dimension we pay is the multiplicative factor
d
. High dimensional additive model (when
d\gg n
) along with some sparsity assumption (namely
sparse additive model
) has also been investigated; initially
[
122
]
established the minimax optimal rate. Subsequent investigations in the literature have delved deeper into sparse additive models (e.g., see
[
99
,
90
,
123
,
158
]
and references therein), expanding upon recent advancements in penalized linear regression. Lastly,
[
140
]
suggests a dual penalty approach, involving the empirical norm and functional semi-norm of each component
f_{\star,j}
to estimate the regression function. Very recently,
[
12
]
extended the analysis for a high dimensional
k
-way sparse interaction model using deep neural networks. However, to the best of our knowledge all the aforementioned results crucially leverage the independence in the DGP and hence cannot be applied to dependent DGPs under appropriate mixing assumptions. To the best of our knowledge,
these are the first results analyzing analyze neural nets in growing dimension under a mixing DGP
. The focus here is on the case when
d
grows with
n
but at a slower rate (see
[
128
]
). The analysis for a high dimensional sparse additive model is left to future research.

We assume the additive noise model (
6.1
) where
\{(X_{i},\xi_{i})\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
forms a
\gamma
-mixing sequence. Also suppose that the
\xi
s are bounded. We now present two sets of assumptions — one on the design and the other on the additive function class.

Assumption 6.2
.

We assume that the covariate
X
is compactly supported on, say
[0,1]^{d}
with continuous and bounded density.

Assumption 6.3
.

Define the function class
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{add}}(d,b,s)
to be set of all functions
f:[0,1]^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
such that:

1.

f
is additive, i.e.
f(x)=\sum_{j}f_{j}(x_{j})

2.

Each component
f_{j}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
f_{j}\in\Sigma(s,b)
i.e.
s
-Hölder function class with Hölder norm bounded by
b
.

3.

\int_{[0,1]}f_{j}(x)\,dx=0
for all
1\leq j\leq d
.

4.

The function
f
is uniformly bounded by
b
.

Hereafter, we assume that
f_{\star}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{add}}(d,b,s)
.

The above conditions are quite standard in the additive model literature and we refer the interested reader to
[
12
, Section 2]
and
[
90
, Section 2]
for similar assumptions in related work. Crucially, they ensure identifiability of the additive model.

We here estimate each component of
f_{\star}
by
d
separate fully connected neural networks each of which has the same structure. To that end, we define

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NN},\mathrm{add}}(d,1,N,L,B):=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{d}f_{j}:\ f_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NN}}(1,1,N,L,B)\right\}.

Finally, we write
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{NN},\mathrm{add}}(d,1,N,L,B)
to be the subset of networks truncated at
2b
. We then define the estimator as follows:

\hat{f}_{n}={\arg\min}_{f\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{NN},\mathrm{add}}(d,1,N,L,B)}\ \left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\left(Y_{i}-f(X_{i})\right)^{2}\right\}\,.

Estimation of additive models in high dimension with i.i.d. sample has been recently explored by
[
12
]
. The following theorem complements their result by establishing the rate of convergence of
\hat{f}
in the presence of dependency among the observations:

Theorem 6.2
.

Suppose
(X_{i},\xi_{i})
is generated from a strictly stationary
\gamma
-mixing sequence with
\gamma_{q}\leq c(1+q)^{-\gamma}
for some
c,\gamma>0
. Also assume that Assumptions
6.2
and
6.3
hold. Let
d\leq N^{s}
for some
0<s<1
. Then the least-square estimator
\hat{f}
satisfies:

\|\hat{f}_{n}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}\left(d^{1-\frac{1}{\gamma}\frac{2s}{2s\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)+1}}n^{-\frac{2s}{2s\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)+1}}\left(\log{(n)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{(1+\gamma)}}\right)\,.

Let us now compare the rate obtained here with that for the i.i.d. case which is
dn^{-2s/(2s+1)}
.
Taking
\gamma\uparrow\infty
, the rate obtained in Theorem
6.2
coincides with the i.i.d. rate and consequently we can recover the minimax optimal rate of estimation for independent observations in the limit. For a fixed
\gamma<\infty
, we unravel an interesting dichotomy; although the power of
n
is compromised due to dependency (it is smaller than what it would have been for the i.i.d. case), the power of
d
is boosted as it is strictly
<1
. At first glance, it may be tempting to think that there is some sort of blessing of dimensionality, i.e. higher the dependence, the lower the effect of dimension on the estimation. However, as we argue now this is not the case. For simplicity, assume
d=n^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha<1
. Then (ignoring the log factor), Theorem
6.2
provides the following rate of convergence:

\|\hat{f}_{n}-f_{0}\|_{2}^{2}\approx O_{p}\left(n^{\alpha-\frac{2s}{2s\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)+1}\left(1+\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}\right)}\right)=O_{p}\left(n^{-\frac{2s(1-\alpha)-\alpha}{2s\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)+1}}\right)\,.

Now, under the same setup, the rate for under i.i.d. observations simplifies to:

\|\hat{f}_{n}-f_{0}\|_{2}^{2}\approx O_{p}(dn^{-\frac{2s}{2s+1}})=O_{p}\left(n^{-\frac{2s(1-\alpha)-\alpha}{2s+1}}\right)\,.

Therefore it is immediate that we overall pay a price for the dependency through a factor of
(1+\gamma)/\gamma
in the rate of convergence. Whether this factor is optimal or not is an intriguing question and is beyond the scope of this paper.

6.3
Shape constrained multivariate convex least squares

Consider a strictly stationary sequence
(X_{1},\xi_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},\xi_{n})
satisfying
\beta/\gamma
-mixing property (specific assumptions to be provided in the results), where marginally
X\sim P_{X}
has a continuous density bounded away from
0
and supported on some compact, convex body
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\xi_{i}
s are assumed bounded. We observe data from the regression model
Y_{i}=f_{\star}(X_{i})+\xi_{i}
,
\mathbb{E}[\xi_{i}|X_{i}]=0
, see (
5.2
), where
f_{\star}
is a convex function on
\Omega
and is bounded by
1
. The goal of this section is to
estimate
f_{\star}
using a natural least squares approach (below) and quantify the rate of convergence under mixing assumptions
instead of the well understood i.i.d. setting.

Nonparametric convex regression has a long history in the statistics
[
74
,
72
,
154
,
61
]
and economics
[
92
,
106
,
148
,
3
]
literature. Our estimator of choice is the bounded convex least squares (BCLS) estimator
[
68
,
133
,
65
]
defined as follows:

\hat{f}_{n}\in{\arg\min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\,\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-f(X_{i}))^{2},

where
\mathcal{F}
is the class of convex functions on
\Omega
bounded by
1
. The above definition fixes
\hat{f}_{n}
only at the design points. It can be extended to the whole of
\omega
by an interpolation technique; see
[
68
, Equations 3.2—3.4]
and
[
133
, Lemmas 2.3 and 2.4]
; also see
Section

C.3
. While the computational and statistical properties of
\hat{f}_{n}
are well understood in the i.i.d. setting, we will show that interestingly the same bounds can be achieved even under long-range dependence provided
d
is large enough. To wit, we focus on polytopal domains
\Omega
, i.e.,

\Omega=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\,a_{i}\leq v_{i}^{\top}x\leq b_{i},\,\mbox{for}\,i=1,2,\ldots,F\},

for some positive integer
F
(not changing with
n
), unit vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{F}
, and real numbers
a_{1},\ldots,a_{F},b_{1},\ldots,b_{F}
. A typical example is
\Omega=[0,1]^{d}
.

Theorem 6.3
.

(a). (Worst case risk) Assume that the DGP is strictly stationary
\beta
-mixing with
\beta_{q}\leq c(1+q)^{-\beta}
for some
c\geq 0
,
\beta>0
.
Then provided
\Omega
is a polytope as described above,
d>4
and
\beta\in(2/(d-2),\infty)\setminus\{1\}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\lVert\hat{f}_{n}-f_{\star}\rVert^{2}_{L_{2}(P_{X})}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2}{d}}(\log{(n)})^{d+1}.

(6.2)

(b). (Adaptation) Assume that the DGP is strictly
\gamma
-mixing with
\gamma_{q}\leq c(1+q)^{-\gamma}
for some
c\geq 0
and
\gamma>0
. Suppose
d>8
,
\gamma>(4/(d-4),\infty)\setminus\{1\}
, and
f_{\star}
,
\Omega
satisfy the following property: there exists
k
simplices
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{k}
such that
\cup_{j=1}^{k}\Delta_{j}
and
f_{\star}
is affine on each
\Delta_{j}
.
we then have

\displaystyle\lVert\hat{f}_{n}-f_{\star}\rVert^{2}_{L_{2}(P_{X})}\,=\,O_{P}\left(n^{-\frac{4}{d}}(\log{(n)})^{d+1}\right).

(6.3)

The above result is interesting from two fronts.

(i) Note that the
n^{-2/d}
rate in part (a) is the optimal achievable rate for the BCLS estimator as matching lower bounds have been established in
[
94
, Theorem 3.1]
. This highlights the optimality of our upper bounds in a worst case sense even under
beta
-mixing type dependence in the long-range regime.

(ii) The adaptation rate of
n^{-4/d}
in part (b) is also known to be the best achievable rate for the BCLS estimator
[
94
, Theorem 2.5]
. This highlights that our bounds are tight enough to recover optimal tuning-free adaptive rates under long-range dependence.

We note that for technical reasons it is necessary to work with BCLS instead of the usual least squares (LS) because the usual convex LS estimator is not well-behaved near the boundary of the domain
\Omega
(see
[
68
, Section 4.1.4]
for details).

Remark 6.3

(On the domain assumption)
.

The polytopal assumption on the domain is due to the fact that both the rates for BCLS and the minimax risk for estimating
f_{\star}
are heavily domain sensitive even in the i.i.d. setting as has been pointed out in numerous influential works
[
93
,
94
,
68
]
. For example, if
\Omega
is a general compact, smooth domain (see
[
68
, Section 1.2.2]
for definition) which includes, then the BCLS has a slower rate of convergence
n^{-2/(d+1)}
for i.i.d. data. Strikingly, this rate is tight and it is achieved, for example, when
P_{X}
is uniform on the
d
-dimensional Euclidean ball (see
[
93
, Remark 2]
,
[
68
, Section 2.2]
). For such domains in the presence of
\gamma
-mixing (as in
Theorem

6.3
, part (b)), we can leverage
5.4
to get the rate
n^{-2/(d+1+2\gamma^{-1})}
which converges (informally) to the minimax optimal rate
n^{-2/(d+1)}
as
\gamma\to\infty
.

6.4
Estimating Wasserstein distances — benefits of regularization under dependence

Given two compactly supported absolutely continuous probability measures
\mu
and
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define the
2
-optimal transport/Wasserstein distance between
\mu
and
\nu
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu):=\min_{\pi\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)}\int\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}\,d\pi(x,y),

(6.4)

where
\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
is the space of all probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the first and second marginals fixed at
\mu
and
\nu
respectively. The existence of an optimizer in (
6.4
) follows from
[
150
, Theorem 4.1]
. The quantity
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)
has attracted a significant amount of attention in recent years with applications in structured prediction
[
53
,
101
]
, image analysis
[
17
,
57
]
, nonparametric testing
[
15
,
37
]
, generative modeling
[
10
,
113
]
, etc. Accordingly, the goal of this section is to estimate
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)
.

In practice, an important hurdle in estimating
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)
is that explicit functional or parametric forms of
\mu
and
\nu
are not usually available, and instead, the practitioner only has access to samples from
\mu
and
\nu
. In other words, suppose that
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
and
\{Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\}
are strictly stationary and
\beta
-mixing with stationary distributions
\mu
and
\nu
respectively. The goal is to estimate
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)
based on the observed samples. The case where
X_{i}
’s and
Y_{j}
’s are independent samples is well understood thanks to many quantitative results in the recent years, see e.g.
[
30
,
35
,
62
,
76
,
104
]
and the references therein. The case of strictly stationary
\beta
-mixing sequences with summable mixing coefficients has also been considered, see e.g.
[
10
,
58
]
, although with different goals in mind. However, to the best of our knowledge, the more interesting case where the
\beta
-mixing coefficients are not summable remains unexplored. In the sequel, we will describe and analyze the two most popular estimators of
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)
which will help us compare the two estimators across different regimes of dependence and dimension
d
.

We now describe the two estimators of
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)
. First, we define

\hat{\mu}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\hat{\nu}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{Y_{j}},

which are the natural empirical measures based on the samples. Both the estimators will be based on the plug-in principle which simply suggests replacing
\mu
and
\nu
with
\hat{\mu}_{n}
and
\hat{\nu}_{n}
respectively, in an appropriate objective function.

•

Standard plug-in estimator
: The first estimator is
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
defined as in (
6.4
). In the sequel, we will provide quantitative finite sample bounds on the quantity

\mathbb{E}|\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)|.

Although
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
is a very natural estimator, it is computationally challenging with a best known worst-case complexity of
\tilde{O}(n^{3})
when
m=n
(up to logarithmic factors); see e.g. the Hungarian algorithm
[
81
]
. This motivates the need for computationally faster estimators with comparable statistical accuracy.

•

Regularized plug-in estimator with Sinkhorn algorithm
: Motivated by the computational issues mentioned above, we follow
[
30
]
and consider the entropic regularized optimal transport problem as a way to estimate
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)
. To wit, consider the entropic optimal transport cost:

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\mu,\nu):=\min_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)}\left(\int\int\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}\,d\gamma(x,y)+2\varepsilon\mathrm{KL}(\gamma|\mu\otimes\nu)\right),

(6.5)

where
\mathrm{KL}(\cdot|\cdot)
denotes the standard Kullback-Leibler divergence, and
\varepsilon>0
. Also define the associated Sinkhorn divergence:

\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}(\mu,\nu)=\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\mu,\nu)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\mu,\mu)+\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\nu,\nu)\right).

(6.6)

Note that
\mathcal{S}_{0,2}(\mu,\nu)=\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)
. It is well-known that the Sinkhorn divergence defined in (
6.6
) is always non-negative
[
54
]
. The plug-in principle would then suggest using
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
with a “small”
\varepsilon
for estimating
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)
. This has inspired a line of work studying (
6.5
) for
\varepsilon\to 0
(see
[
36
,
27
,
120
]
). A major breakthrough for solving the optimization problem in (
6.5
) can be attributed to
[
31
]
where the author proposed the Sinkhorn algorithm or IPFP (iterative proportional fitting procedure) for solving (
6.5
); also see
[
1
,
46
]
for related work. As (
6.5
) cannot be solved explicitly, we will instead construct our estimator based on an appropriate number of Sinkhorn iterates as described below.

Set
v_{j}^{(0)}=0
for
j\in[n]
and for
k\geq 1
,
i\in[m]
, let

u_{i}^{(k)}:=-\varepsilon\log\left(n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\sf exp}\left(\varepsilon^{-1}(v_{j}^{(k-1)}-\lVert X_{i}-Y_{j}\rVert^{2})\right)\right),\quad\mbox{and}

v_{j}^{(k)}:=-\varepsilon\log\left(m^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\sf exp}\left(\varepsilon^{-1}(u_{i}^{(k)}-\lVert X_{i}-Y_{j}\rVert^{2})\right)\right).

Then the approximation for the estimator
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
after
k
iterations of the Sinkhorn algorithm is given by
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}^{(k)}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n}):=2m^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}u_{i}^{(k)}+n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}v_{j}^{(k)}
. One can similarly define
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}^{(k)}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
as in (
6.6
) with
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
replaced by
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}^{(k)}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
. We shall use
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}^{(k)}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
as our estimator for
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)
for an appropriately chosen
k
and
\varepsilon
, and provide finite sample bounds on

\mathbb{E}\big|\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}^{(k)}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})\big|.

The goal of this section is to address the following question:

How do the above estimators
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
and
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}^{(k)}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
compare based on their statistical accuracy and computational complexity in the presence of
\beta
-mixing?

Theorem 6.4
.

Assume that
\mu
and
\nu
are compactly supported on a convex set, absolutely continuous distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Fix some
d\geq 4
and say
\beta_{q}\leq C(1+q)^{-\beta}
for some
\beta,C>0
,
q\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Then the following conclusions hold:

(I)

The unregularized estimator
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\Bigg|\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\bigg|\lesssim\begin{cases}\hfill n^{-\frac{2}{d}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta>\frac{2}{d-2}\\
n^{-\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta<\frac{2}{d-2}\end{cases}.

(6.7)

(II)

Next suppose that both
\mu
and
\nu
have a finite Fisher information. Also assume that the Fisher information of the Wasserstein geodesic between
\mu
and
\nu
is finite (for all relevant technical definitions, see
Section

C.4
). The regularized estimator
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}^{(k)}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
then satisfies

\mathbb{E}\Bigg|\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon_{n},2}^{(k_{n})}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\bigg|\lesssim\begin{cases}\hfill n^{-\frac{2}{d}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta>\frac{2}{d-2}\\
n^{-\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta<\frac{2}{d-2}\end{cases},

(6.8)

where

(k_{n},\varepsilon_{n})=\begin{cases}\hfill\left(n^{\frac{3}{d}},n^{-\frac{1}{d}}\right)&\mbox{if}\ \beta>\frac{2}{d-2}\\
\left(n^{\frac{3\beta}{2(\beta+1)}},n^{-\frac{\beta}{2(\beta+1)}}\right)&\mbox{if}\ \beta<\frac{2}{d-2}\end{cases}.

With the above choices of
(k_{n},\varepsilon_{n})
, it follows that
\mathcal{S}^{(k_{n})}_{\varepsilon_{n},2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
can be computed in time
\tilde{O}(n^{2+\frac{3}{d}})
for
\beta>2/(d-2)
, and in time
\tilde{O}(n^{2+\frac{3\beta}{2(\beta+1)}})
for
\beta<2/(d-2)
.

Remark 6.4

(Benefits of entropic regularization under long-range dependence)
.

Based on
Theorem

6.4
, we note that both
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
and
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}^{(k)}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
attain the same statistical upper bounds for estimating
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)
. On the other hand, there is a marked difference in their computational complexities. As mentioned before, the worst case complexity for computing
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
is
\tilde{O}(n^{3})
. Note that the computational complexity of
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}^{(k)}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
is always faster than
\tilde{O}(n^{3})
. In particular, informally speaking, as
\beta
gets smaller with fixed
d
, the complexity of the regularized estimator
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}^{(k)}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
also decreases and approaches
\tilde{O}(n^{2})
. Similarly, if
d
increases for fixed
\beta
, then again the computational complexity of
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}^{(k)}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
decreases and approaches
\tilde{O}(n^{2})
. This suggests that
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}^{(k)}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
can be a more favorable estimator than
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})
, particularly under strong dependence or large values of
d
. We also note that the benefits of regularization for large
d
was already pointed out in
[
30
]
. Our main contribution in this section is to extract the benefits of dependence.

Remark 6.5

(Assumptions on
\mu
,
\nu
)
.

There are a number of natural extensions of
Theorem

6.4
possible with different assumptions on
\mu
,
\nu
, and other cost functions beyond the squared distance. A particularly interesting one is when say, one of
\mu
or
\nu
, is supported on a discrete set of bounded size (in
n
). In this case, using the same proof technique as employed for proving
Theorem

6.4
, coupled with
[
76
, Lemma 2.1]
, it is possible to show

\mathbb{E}\Bigg|\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\bigg|\lesssim\begin{cases}\hfill n^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta>1\\
n^{-\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta<1\end{cases}.

6.5
Classification under Mammen-Tsybakov margin condition

In classification problems, one observes
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})
where each
(X_{i},Y_{i})\sim P
(unknown), a probability measure supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{0,1\}
. We will write the marginal of
X
as
P_{X}
and we refer to
functions
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\{0,1\}
as classifiers
. The goal is to find
g
that minimizes the
generalization error
defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(g):=\mathbb{P}(Y\neq g(X))-\inf_{g}\mathbb{P}(Y\neq g(X)).

(6.9)

It is well known that the infimum above is attained by the Bayes optimal classifier
g_{\star}(x):=\mathbf{1}(\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x]\geq 1/2)
(see
[
40
]
). As
P
is unknown, neither
\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x]
nor
\mathcal{E}(\cdot)
can be computed explicitly.

There are two predominant approaches to tackling the classification problem — (a) Plug-in principle where the strategy is to estimate
\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x]
under smoothness assumptions (see), and (b) ERM where
\mathcal{E}_{P}(\cdot)
is replaced by its empirical analog
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{n}}(\cdot)
; see
[
40
,
147
,
142
,
2
]
and the references therein. In keeping with the rest of the paper, we will adopt the latter approach here and relax the standard i.i.d. assumption by allowing for dependence in the DGP via mixing assumptions.

As the second term in (
6.9
) is free of
g
, a natural estimator can be constructed by minimizing the empirical training error, i.e.,

\hat{g}_{n}\in{\arg\min}_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\,\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}(Y_{i}\neq g(X_{i})),

where
\mathcal{G}
is some prespecified hypothesis class. We assume
g_{\star}\in\mathcal{G}
to avoid digressions. The global ERM defined above has been studied previously in the i.i.d. setting; see e.g.,
[
55
,
105
,
89
,
142
]
but to the best of our knowledge, no quantitative bounds on the generalization error
\mathcal{E}_{P}(\hat{g}_{n})
exists when the independence assumption in the DGP is removed. The result below aims to fill this gap in the literature.

Before stating the main result, we require an important assumption, namely the “Mammen-Tsybakov margin (low noise) condition” (see
[
103
,
142
]
): there exists
c>0
such that

\mathcal{E}(g)\geq c\lVert g-g_{\star}\rVert_{L_{2}(P_{X})}^{2}.

(6.10)

Following the notation of
[
142
]
, (
6.10
) assumes the margin condition with
\kappa=2
. We refer the reader to the discussion around
[
142
, Proposition 1]
for motivation on the margin condition and its relation with the behavior of
\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x]
near
1/2
. For larger values of
\kappa
, it is possible to derive faster rates (even under dependence) when
\mathcal{G}
is of low complexity (see
[
142
,
55
]
). However to keep the discussion streamlined, we only focus on
\mathcal{G}
of high complexity and
\kappa=1
in the following result.

Theorem 6.5
.

Suppose that the DGP is stationary
\gamma
-mixing with
\gamma_{q}\leq C(1+q)^{-\gamma}
for some
C>0
. Assume that
\mathcal{G}:=\{\mathbf{1}(C):\ C\in\mathcal{C}\}
and
\mathcal{C}
satisfies the following entropy condition: there exists
\alpha>1+\gamma^{-1}
such that for all
u>0
,
\mathscr{H}_{[\,]}(u,\{\mathbf{1}(C):\ C\in\mathcal{C}\},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{1})\leq Lu^{-\alpha}
. Finally assume that
\mathcal{G}
satisfies the margin condition (
6.10
). Then given any
\{r_{n}\}){n\geq 1}
such that
r_{n}^{2}n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1+\gamma^{-1}}}\to\infty
, we have

\mathcal{E}(\hat{g}_{n})=o_{P}(r_{n}^{2}).

Note that as
\gamma\to\infty
, the generalization error informally converges to
n^{-1/(\alpha+1)}
which is the minimax optimal rate in the i.i.d. setting (see
[
66
]
). This shows that our empirical process bounds can be used to derive meaning rates even with non-smooth loss functions. The presentation and proof of
Theorem

6.5
follows that of
[
66
, Theorem 3.5]
and
[
55
, Theorem 7.1]
with appropriate modifications to incorporate dependence. We also refer the reader to the discussion around
[
103
, Theorem 1]
for examples of
\mathcal{G}
that satisfy the entropy condition in
Theorem

6.5
.

7
Conclusion and future research

In this paper, we provide the first quantifiable trade-offs between dependence among the observed data and the complexity of function/hypothesis classes.
A major finding is that i.i.d. like rates hold under long-range dependence for complex enough function classes.
Our approach is broadly applicable as we provide dependence-sensitive bounds on empirical processes across all levels of dependence (both long and short-range), which can be applied directly to various learning problems of interest (See Section
6
for some applications).
Several interesting future research directions originate from our work.

•

Minimax lower bounds
— A natural question is whether our trade-offs are optimal in a minimax sense. A first step towards that should be to construct sequences for which the covariance inequality in
Lemma

A.2
is tight. It seems that an extension of the lower bound in
[
124
, Theorem 1.1 (b)]
to infinite sequences would be helpful.

•

Concentration inequality
— While we obtain upper bounds for the expected supremum of empirical processes, the question of obtaining Talagrand type concentration inequalities (see
[
139
,
18
]
) under polynomial dependence remains open. In this case, we do not expect sub-exponential decay and it seems challenging to extract the correct polynomial tail without enforcing strong model assumptions or without restricting to VC classes of functions.

•

Extension to other notions of dependency
— While our results apply to
\beta/\gamma
mixing sequences, it remains challenging to establish same bounds for weaker notions of dependence, such as
\alpha
-mixing
[
125
]
,
\tau
-mixing
[
38
]
, nonlinear system theory based dependence
[
153
]
, etc.

A
Proof of main results

This section is organized as follows: In
Section

A.1
, we present some preparatory lemmas which will be used in the proofs of our main results. The proofs of Theorems
2.1
,
3.1
,
4.1
, and
5.1
will be presented in Sections
A.2
,
A.3
,
A.4
, and
A.5
.

A.1
Auxiliary Lemmas

In this section, we collect all the auxiliary results needed for our main theorems. The proofs of these results are deferred to
Section

A.1
. We would also refer the reader to
Section

1.3
for all the notation used in the sequel.

The first result relates two important norms
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2,P}
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\phi,P}
for
\phi\in\Phi
.

Lemma A.1
.

Given a measurable
h:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
and probability measure
P
supported on
\mathcal{X}
such that
h\in\mathcal{L}_{\phi}^{P}
, for
\phi\in\Phi
, there exists a constant
c_{\phi}>1
such that

\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{2,P}\leq c_{\phi}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}.

The next lemma provides a generic variance bound under a
\beta
-mixing assumption.

Lemma A.2
.

Suppose
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a stationary
\beta
-mixing sequence with marginal distribution
P
, taking values in some Polish space
\mathcal{X}
. Recall the definition of
\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}
from (
2.3
). Let
h:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\phi\in\Phi
, and
c_{\phi}
be taken from
Lemma

A.1
. Then

{\sf var}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{q}h(X_{i})\right)\leq q\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}^{2}\left(c_{\phi}^{2}+2\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(q)\right)\,.

We will now state an important maximal inequality for a finite set of functions which plays a very important role in the proof of
Theorem

2.1
.

Proposition A.1
.

Consider the same setting as in
Lemma

A.2
. Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\phi}(P)
be a finite collection of functions
f
with
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq b
and
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\phi,P}\leq\sigma
for some
b,\sigma>0
and all
f\in\mathcal{F}
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(f(X_{i})-Pf\right)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq K\inf_{1\leq q\leq n}\left(\sigma\pi_{\phi,\beta}(q)\sqrt{1+\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}+bq\frac{1+\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}{\sqrt{n}}+b\beta_{q}\sqrt{n}\right),

for some absolute constant
K>0
, where

\pi_{\phi,\beta}(q):=\sqrt{c_{\phi}^{2}+2\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(q)}.

(A.1)

The next result is a technical bound for the upper tail
L_{1}(P)
integral of a function and is a generalization of

[
43
, Lemma 4]
. We need to define two relevant quantities. Fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
q\in\mathbb{N}
, and
h\in L_{1}(P)
. Recall that
Q_{h}
is the inverse of
t\mapsto P(|f(X)|>t\cdot)
and define,

B_{q}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\bigg(\sum_{k=0}^{q}\mathds{1}(u\leq\beta(k))\bigg)\,du=\sum_{k=0}^{q}(\beta(k)\wedge t)\,,

(A.2)

and

\omega_{q,h}(\varepsilon):=\sup_{t\leq\varepsilon}\ Q_{h}(t)\sqrt{B_{q}(t)}.

(A.3)

We are now ready to state the required result.

Lemma A.3
.

Fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
q\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
h\in L_{1}(P)
. Then

\displaystyle\lVert h\mathds{1}(|h|>Q_{h}(\varepsilon))\rVert_{L_{1}(P)}\leq\frac{2\varepsilon\omega_{q,h}(\varepsilon)}{\sqrt{B_{q}(\varepsilon)}}.

(A.4)

Suppose now
h\in\mathcal{L}_{\phi}(P)
and
\upsilon>0
satisfies

\displaystyle\upsilon\sqrt{\varepsilon\sum_{k=0}^{q}\mathds{1}(\varepsilon\leq\beta_{k})}\geq\lVert h\rVert_{\phi,P}\sqrt{\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(q)}.

(A.5)

Then, we have:

\displaystyle\lVert h\mathds{1}(|h|>\upsilon)\rVert_{L_{1}(P)}\leq 2\lVert h\rVert_{\phi,P}\sqrt{\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(q)}\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sum_{k=0}^{q}\mathds{1}(\varepsilon\leq\beta_{k})}}.

(A.6)

A.2
Proof of
Theorem

2.1

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we will use
M
to denote universal constants that might change from line to line. In order to extend
A.1
to the case where
|\mathcal{F}|
is infinite, we need further notation.

First, we define

S:=\min\left\{s\geq 0:2^{-s}\leq\frac{a}{2^{4}\sqrt{n}\sigma}\right\}.

By (
2.6
), we have

\frac{a}{2^{4}\sqrt{n}\sigma}\leq\frac{1}{2},

and so
S\geq 1
, and
\sigma>2^{-(S+1)}\sigma
. By definition of
S
,
2^{-(S-1)}>a/(2^{4}\sqrt{n}\sigma)
and so
\sigma>2^{-(S+1)}\sigma>a/(2^{6}\sqrt{n})
. This implies:

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{1+\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({u},\mathcal{F})}\,du

\displaystyle\geq\int_{2^{-(S+1)}\sigma}^{\sigma}\sqrt{1+\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({u},\mathcal{F})}\,du

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{S+1}\int_{2^{-s}\sigma}^{2^{-s+1}\sigma}\sqrt{1+\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({u},\mathcal{F})}\,du

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{s=1}^{S+1}2^{-s}\sigma\sqrt{1+\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({2^{-s+1}\sigma},\mathcal{F})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{s=0}^{S}2^{-s}\sigma\sqrt{1+H_{s}},

where
H_{s}:=\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({2^{-s}\sigma},\mathcal{F})
,
s=0,1,\ldots,S
. Above, we have used the non-increasing nature of
\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({\cdot},\mathcal{F})
in the first argument. By (
2.6
) and noting that

R_{n,\beta}^{(1)}(\delta)\geq 1+\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({\delta},\mathcal{F})

for
\delta>0
, we then have:

\sum_{s=0}^{S}2^{-s}\sigma\sqrt{1+H_{s}}\leq\frac{2a}{C_{0}}.

(A.7)

An implication of (
A.7
) is also that:

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{S}2^{-s}\sigma\sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{s}(1+H_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{S}\sqrt{1+H_{k}}\sigma\sum_{s=k}^{S}2^{-s}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{k=0}^{S}2^{-k}\sigma\sqrt{1+H_{k}}\leq\frac{4a}{C_{0}}.

(A.8)

Here the first inequality uses the fact that
\sqrt{\sum_{j}a_{j}}\leq\sum_{j}\sqrt{a_{j}}
.
Similarly, by using the non-increasing nature of
R_{n,\beta}^{(1)}
, we also have:

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{S}2^{-s}\sigma\sqrt{R_{n,\beta}^{(1)}(2^{-s}\sigma)}\leq\frac{2a}{C_{0}}.

(A.9)

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{S}2^{-s}\sigma\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-s}\sigma})}\sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{s}(1+H_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{S}\sqrt{1+H_{k}}\sigma\sum_{s=k}^{S}2^{-s}\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-s}\sigma})}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{k=0}^{S}2^{-k}\sigma\sqrt{1+H_{k}}\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-k}\sigma})}\leq\frac{4a}{C_{0}}.

(A.10)

In the last display above, we used the non-decreasing nature of
\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({\cdot})
from
2.2
, followed by (
2.5
) and (
A.9
).

Keeping the above observations in mind, for
s=0,1,\ldots,S
, let
[g_{j}^{s,L},g_{j}^{s,U}]_{j=1}^{N_{s}}
be a
2^{-s}\sigma
-bracket of
\mathcal{F}
, where
N_{s}=\mathscr{N}_{[\,]}(2^{-s}\sigma,\mathcal{F},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\phi})
is the
2^{-s}\sigma
-bracketing number of
\mathcal{F}
. Given any
f\in\mathcal{F}
, and any
s\in\{0,1,\ldots,S\}
, there exists
[g_{j_{f}}^{s,L},g_{j_{f}}^{s,U}]
such that

g_{j_{f}}^{s,L}(x)\leq f(x)\leq g_{j_{f}}^{s,U}(x),\,\mbox{for all}\ x\in\mathcal{X}
, and
\lVert g_{j_{f}}^{s,U}-g_{j_{f}}^{s,L}\rVert_{L_{1}(P)}\leq 2^{-s}\sigma
.

Further define the following finer (and nested) brackets:

\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(x):=\max_{0\leq k\leq s}g_{j_{f}}^{k,L}(x),\quad\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,U}(x):=\min_{0\leq k\leq s}g_{j_{f}}^{k,U}(x),

and

\Delta_{f}^{s}(x):=\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,U}(x)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(x).

It is immediate from the definition that:

\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(x)\leq f(x)\leq\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,U}(x).

(A.11)

Define

\displaystyle K_{s}:=\frac{2^{-s+1}\sqrt{n}\sigma\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-s-1}\sigma})}}{\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma)\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s+1}H_{k}}},\quad\mbox{for}\ 0\leq s\leq S-1.

(A.12)

Finally, define

\displaystyle\nu_{f}(x):=\begin{cases}\min\{0\leq s\leq S-1:\ \Delta_{f}^{s}(x)\geq K_{s}\}&\mbox{if}\ \Delta_{f}^{s}(x)\geq K_{s}\ \mbox{for some}\ 0\leq s\leq S-1,\\
S&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(A.13)

With this in view, given any
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we begin with the following decomposition:

\displaystyle f(x)=\tilde{g}_{f}^{0,L}(x)+\sum_{s=0}^{S}(f(x)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(x))\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(x)=s)+\sum_{s=1}^{S}(\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(x)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s-1,L}(x))\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(x)\geq s),

(A.14)

which is easy to verify. For notational convenience, define:

\begin{split}\Theta^{(1)}_{f,s}(x)&:=(f(x)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(x))\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(x)=s),\\
\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(x)&:=(\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(x)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s-1,L}(x))\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(x)\geq s).\end{split}

(A.15)

Consequently the following bound holds:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}f(X_{1}))\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tilde{g}_{f}^{0,L}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\tilde{g}_{f}^{0,L}(X_{1}))\bigg|\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{s=0}^{S}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Theta^{(1)}_{f,s}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\Theta^{(1)}_{f,s}(X_{1})\big)\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{s=1}^{S}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(X_{1})\big)\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle=:T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}.

We now bound the three terms
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
, and
T_{3}
individually.

Bound for
T_{1}
:
As
f\in\mathcal{F}
varies, note that
\tilde{g}_{f}^{0,L}
varies over a finite set of cardinality
{\sf exp}(H_{0})
. With this observation, we bound
T_{1}
directly using
Lemma

A.2
to get:

T_{1}\leq M\inf_{1\leq q\leq n}\left(\sigma\pi_{\phi,\beta}(q)\sqrt{1+H_{0}}+bq\frac{1+H_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}+b\beta_{q}\sqrt{n}\right)

(A.16)

for the same constant as in
Lemma

A.2
. We now plug in
q\equiv\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\sigma)
in (
A.16
) and observe that:

b\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\sigma)\frac{1+H_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}+b\beta(\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\sigma))\sqrt{n}\leq 2b\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\sigma)\frac{1+H_{0}}{n}.

Combining the above observations with (
A.7
), (
A.9
) and (
A.16
), we get:

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\leq M\left(\sigma c_{\phi}\sqrt{1+H_{0}}+2\sigma\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({\sigma})(1+H_{0})}+2b\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\sigma)\frac{1+H_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{M\left(\frac{2ac_{\phi}}{C_{0}}+\frac{4a}{C_{0}}+2b\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\sigma)\frac{1+H_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\leq\frac{2aM}{C_{0}}(c_{\phi}+4)+2bM\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\sigma)\frac{1+H_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}}.

(A.17)

Bound on
T_{2}
:

Recall the definition of
T_{2}
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{s=0}^{S}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Theta^{(1)}_{f,s}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\Theta^{(1)}_{f,s}(X_{1})\big)\bigg|\right]

where
\Theta^{(1)}_{f,s}(x)=(f(x)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(x))\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(x)=s)
. It is immediate that:

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Theta^{(1)}_{f,0}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\Theta^{(1)}_{f,0}(X_{1})\big)\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{s=1}^{S}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Theta^{(1)}_{f,s}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\Theta^{(1)}_{f,s}(X_{1})\big)\bigg|\right]

(A.18)

Note that, by (
A.11
), we have the following for any
0\leq s\leq S
:

\displaystyle\Theta^{(1)}_{f,s}(X)-\mathbb{E}[\Theta^{(1)}_{f,s}(X)]

\displaystyle=(f(X)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(X))\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X)=s)-\mathbb{E}\left[(f(X)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(X))\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X)=s)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Delta_{f}^{s}(X)\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X)=s)-\mathbb{E}\left[(f(X)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(X))\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X)=s)\right]

\displaystyle=\Delta_{f}^{s}(X)\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X)=s)-\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{f}^{s}(X)\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X)=s)]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{\Delta_{f}^{s}(X)-(f(X)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(X))\right\}\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X)=s)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Delta_{f}^{s}(X)\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X)=s)-\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{f}^{s}(X)\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X)=s)]+\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{f}^{s}(X)\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X)=s)]

Therefore from (
A.2
), we have:

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{S}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{i})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{i})=s)-\mathbb{E}\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=s)\big)\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Delta_{f}^{0}(X_{i})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{i})=0)-\mathbb{E}\Delta_{f}^{0}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=0)\big)\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle+\sqrt{n}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}\big|\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=s)\big)\big|+\sqrt{n}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}\big|\Delta_{f}^{S}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=S)\big)\big|\,

\displaystyle=:T_{2,1}+T_{2,2}+T_{2,3}+T_{2,4}.

(A.19)

We now bound each of the four terms above. Towards this direction, define a function class

\mathcal{F}_{s}:=\left\{\Delta_{f}^{s}\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}=s):f\in\mathcal{F}\right\}.

We then have the following observations:

|\mathcal{F}_{s}|\leq\left(\Pi_{k=0}^{s}N_{k}\right)^{2}\implies\log{|\mathcal{F}_{s}|}\leq 2\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}.

(A.20)

\lVert\Delta_{f}^{0}\rVert_{\infty}\leq 2b.

(A.21)

By the definition of
\nu_{f}
,

\left\|\Delta_{f}^{s}\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}=s)\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|\Delta_{f}^{s-1}\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}=s)\right\|_{\infty}\leq K_{s-1}\quad\mbox{for}\,s=1,\ldots,S.

(A.22)

\left\|\Delta_{f}^{s}\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}=s)\right\|_{\phi,P}\leq\left\|\Delta_{f}^{s}\right\|_{\phi,P}\leq 2^{-s}\sigma.

(A.23)

To bound
T_{2}
, we begin with the first term on the right hand side of (
A.2
). Again, as application of maximal inequality for finite
\mathcal{F}
from
A.1
coupled with (
A.20
), (
A.22
), and (
A.23
), yields:

\displaystyle T_{2,1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{S}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{i})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{i})=s)-\mathbb{E}\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=s)\big)\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle\leq M\sum_{s=1}^{S}\inf_{1\leq q\leq n}\left(2^{-s}\sigma\pi_{\phi,\beta}(q)\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}+K_{s-1}q\frac{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}{\sqrt{n}}+K_{s-1}\beta_{q}\sqrt{n}\right)

(A.24)

For
1\leq s\leq S
, by choosing
q\equiv\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s}\sigma)
, we observe that

q\frac{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}{\sqrt{n}}+\beta_{q}\sqrt{n}\leq 2\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s}\sigma)\frac{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}{\sqrt{n}},

and

K_{s-1}\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s}\sigma)\frac{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}{\sqrt{n}}=2^{-s+2}\sigma\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-s}\sigma})}\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}.

Combining the two displays above with (
A.2
), (
A.2
), and (
A.2
), we get the following:

\displaystyle T_{2,1}

\displaystyle\leq M\sum_{s=1}^{S}\bigg(2^{-s}\sigma c_{\phi}\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}+2^{-s+4}\sigma\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-s}\sigma})}\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{M\left(\frac{4ac_{\phi}}{C_{0}}+\frac{64a}{C_{0}}\right)=\frac{4aM}{C_{0}}(c_{\phi}+16)}.

(A.25)

We move on to the second term on the right hand side of (
A.2
). By invoking
A.1
combined with (
A.21
), we have:

\displaystyle T_{2,2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Delta_{f}^{0}(X_{i})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{i})=0)-\mathbb{E}\Delta_{f}^{0}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=0)\big)\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle\leq M\inf_{1\leq q\leq n}\left(\sigma\pi_{\phi,\beta}(q)\sqrt{1+H_{0}}+2bq\frac{1+H_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}+2b\beta_{q}\sqrt{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{\frac{4aM}{C_{0}}(c_{\phi}+4)+4bMq\frac{1+H_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}},

where the last bound follows from (
A.2
) by choosing
q\equiv\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\sigma)
.

We now move on to the third term on the right hand side of (
A.2
). The main technical tool for bounding this term, i.e.
T_{2,3}
, is
Lemma

A.3
. We invoke it with the following choices of parameters:

\upsilon\equiv\upsilon_{s}:=K_{s}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\varepsilon\equiv\varepsilon_{s}:=((\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma)-1)\vee 1)\frac{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s+1}H_{k}}{n}.

By the definition of
\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s}\sigma)
, we have

\beta(\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma)-1)\geq\varepsilon_{s}.

In other words, we have:

\sum_{k=0}^{\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma)}\mathds{1}(\varepsilon_{s}\leq\beta(k))\geq(\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma)-1)\vee 1\geq\frac{1}{2}\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma).

Using the above displays, we conclude that:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\upsilon\sqrt{\varepsilon_{s}\sum_{k=0}^{\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma)}\mathds{1}(\varepsilon_{s}\leq\beta(k))}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-s+1}\sqrt{n}\sigma\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-s-1}\sigma})}}{\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma)\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s+1}H_{k}}}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{s}}\sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma)}\mathds{1}(\varepsilon_{s}\leq\beta(k))}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2^{-s+1}\sqrt{n}\sigma\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-s-1}\sigma})}}{\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma)\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s+1}H_{k}}}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma)\big(1+\sum_{k=0}^{s+1}H_{k}\big)}{2n}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma)}

\displaystyle=\cdot 2^{-s}\sigma\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-s-1}\sigma})}

\displaystyle\geq\lVert\Delta_{f}^{s}\rVert_{\phi,P}\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-s-1}\sigma})}

\displaystyle\geq\lVert\Delta_{f}^{s}\rVert_{\phi,P}\sqrt{\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma))}.

Here the last two inequalities follow from (
A.23
) and (
2.4
) respectively. The above inequality verifies (
A.5
) with

q\equiv\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma),\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad h\equiv\Delta_{f}^{s}.

Therefore by applying (
A.6
), combined with (
A.23
), we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big|\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=s)\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-s+1}\sigma\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-s-1}\sigma})}\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon_{s}}{\sum_{k=0}^{\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(2^{-s-1}\sigma)}\mathds{1}(\varepsilon_{s}\leq\beta(k))}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}2^{-s+1}\sigma\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-s-1}\sigma})}\sqrt{\frac{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s+1}H_{k}}{n}}.

With the above observation, we can now bound
T_{2,3}
as follows:

\displaystyle T_{2,3}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}\big|\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=s)\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{8\sqrt{n}\sum_{s=1}^{S}2^{-s}\sigma\sqrt{\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({2^{-s}\sigma})}\sqrt{\frac{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}{n}}\leq\frac{64a}{C_{0}}},

where the final two inequalities follow from (
2.5
) and (
A.2
) respectively.

Finally, we arrive at the fourth term on the right hand side of (
A.2
). Note that, by using
Lemma

A.1
and (
A.23
), we have:

\displaystyle T_{2,4}=\sqrt{n}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}\big|\Delta_{f}^{S}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=S)\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq c_{\phi}\sqrt{n}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\|\Delta_{f}^{S}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=S)\big)\|_{\phi,P}

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{\sqrt{n}2^{-S}\sigma\leq\frac{ac_{\phi}}{2^{4}}}\,.

Here the last bound follows from the definition of
S
.

Combining all the bounds, we get:

T_{2}\leq\boxed{\frac{aM}{16}\left(c_{\phi}+\frac{c_{\phi}+1}{C_{0}}\right)+bMq\frac{1+H_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}}\,.

Bound on
T_{3}
:
Recall the definition of
T_{3}
:

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{s=1}^{S}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(X_{1})\big)\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{S}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(X_{1})\big)\bigg|\right]

where
\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(x)=(\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(x)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s-1,L}(x))\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(x)\geq s)
. Following similar argument as used in
T_{2}
, we have:

1.

|\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s}|\leq\left(\Pi_{k=0}^{s}N_{k}\right)^{2}\implies\log{|\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s}|}\leq 2\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}
.

2.

|\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(x)|\leq K_{s-1}
.

3.

\|\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(X)\|_{\phi,P}\leq 2^{-(s-1)}\sigma
.

Therefore, again applying the maximal inequality for the finite collection of functions, we have:

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle\;\leq M\sum_{s=1}^{S}\inf_{1\leq q\leq n}\left(2^{-(s-1)}\sigma\pi_{\phi,\beta}(q)\sqrt{1+2\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}+K_{s-1}q\frac{1+2\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}{\sqrt{n}}+K_{s-1}\beta_{q}\sqrt{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{\frac{8aM}{C_{0}}(c_{\phi}+16)}\,,

where the last bound follows from (
A.2
). Combining the bounds for
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
, and
T_{3}
, completes the proof.
∎

A.3
Proof of Theorem
3.1

The proof of this Theorem is very similar to that of
Theorem

2.1
. We only highlight the key differences here.
In particular, we need slightly different forms of two results used earlier, namely
A.1
and
Lemma

A.3
.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a stationary
\beta
-mixing sequence with marginal distribution
P
, taking values in some Polish space
\mathcal{X}
. Also let
\mathcal{F}
be a finite collection of functions
f
with
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq\sigma
for some
\sigma>0
and all
f\in\mathcal{F}
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(f(X_{i})-Pf\right)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq K\sigma\inf_{1\leq q\leq n}\left(\pi_{\infty,\beta}(q)\sqrt{1+\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}+q\frac{1+\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}{\sqrt{n}}+\beta_{q}\sqrt{n}\right),

for some absolute constant
K>0
, where

\pi_{\infty,\beta}(q):=\sqrt{1+2\sum_{k=0}^{q}\beta_{k}}.

(A.26)

Lemma A.4
.

Fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
q\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
h\in L_{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
. Recall the definition of
Q_{h}
,
B_{q}
, and
\omega_{q,h}
from
Section

1.3
, (
A.2
), and (
A.3
) respectively. Then

\displaystyle\lVert h\mathds{1}(|h|>Q_{h}(\varepsilon))\rVert_{L_{1}(P)}\leq\frac{2\varepsilon\omega_{q,h}(\varepsilon)}{\sqrt{B_{q}(\varepsilon)}}.

(A.27)

Suppose now
\upsilon>0
satisfies

\displaystyle\upsilon\sqrt{\varepsilon\sum_{k=0}^{q}\mathds{1}(\varepsilon\leq\beta_{k})}\geq\lVert h\rVert_{\infty,\mathcal{X}}\sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{q}\beta_{k}}.

(A.28)

Then, we have:

\displaystyle\lVert h\mathds{1}(|h|>\upsilon)\rVert_{L_{1}(P)}\leq 2\lVert h\rVert_{\infty,\mathcal{X}}\sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{q}\beta_{k}}\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sum_{k=0}^{q}\mathds{1}(\varepsilon\leq\beta_{k})}}.

(A.29)

Let us now complete the proof of
Theorem

3.1
using the above results. We proceed exactly as in the proof of
Theorem

2.1
by decomposing an upper bound on the LHS of (
1.1
) into terms
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
,
T_{3}
, and further decomposing
T_{2}
into
\{T_{2,i}\}_{i\in[4]}
. Instead of invoking
A.1
for bounding
T_{1}
,
T_{2,1}
,
T_{2,2}
and
T_{3}
, we will now use
A.2
. Similarly, instead of invoking
Lemma

A.3
to bound
T_{2,3}
, we will now use
Lemma

A.4
. The bound on
T_{2,4}
stays exactly the same. The rest of the proof is verbatim that of
Theorem

2.1
. We leave the rest of the bookkeeping to the reader.

A.4
Proof of Theorem
4.1

In this section, we provide a proof of Theorem
4.1
. While we will still use the blocking and adaptive truncation strategy that was used for the proof of Theorem
2.1
, a more careful chaining is required to balance all the required contributions. We begin with a modified version of
A.2
.

Proposition A.3
.

Suppose
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a stationary
\gamma
-mixing sequence with marginal distribution
P
supported on some Polish space
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\mathcal{F}

be a finite collection of functions on
\mathcal{X}
with
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq b
and
\|f\|_{r}\leq\sigma
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
, for some
\sigma,b>0
,
r\geq 1
. Define
\tilde{r}:=r\wedge 2
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(f(X_{i})-Pf\right)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq K\inf_{1\leq q\leq n}\left(b^{1-\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{\sigma^{\tilde{r}}\sum_{k=1}^{q-1}\gamma_{k}}\sqrt{1+\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}+bq\frac{1+\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}{\sqrt{n}}+b\gamma_{q}\sqrt{n}\right),

for some absolute constant
K>0
.

We will begin with the same construction of a nested sequence of brackets as in the proof of Theorem
2.1
. Fix
S\geq 0
(to be chosen later). Throughout this proof, the generic constant
C
and the sign
\lesssim
will be used to denote changing constants depending only on
\alpha,\gamma
,
r
, and
V
. We begin with some preliminary observations. Define
H_{k}:=\mathbb{H}_{2}(2^{-k}\sigma,\mathcal{F})
where

\mathbb{H}_{2}(u,\mathcal{F}):=D\left(\log\left(\frac{B}{u}\right)\right)^{V}\left(\frac{\theta}{u}\right)^{\alpha},

for some
B\geq b\vee e\vee\sigma
,
\theta\geq\sigma
,
V,\alpha\geq 0
, and
D\geq 1
. First note that for
s\in[S]
, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\sim D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)\left[\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}+s^{V}\right].

(A.30)

Next, note that whenever
1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\leq n
, then by (
4.1
) and (
A.30
), we have:

\displaystyle\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(2^{-s}\sigma)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\frac{n}{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma+1}}

(A.31)

\displaystyle\lesssim 2\left(\frac{n\sigma^{\alpha}}{D\theta^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma}}\left[\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{-\frac{V}{1+\gamma}}+s^{-\frac{V}{1+\gamma}}\right].

As a result, we get:

\displaystyle\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(2^{-s}\sigma)\begin{cases}\lesssim 2\left(\frac{n\sigma^{\alpha}}{D\theta^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma}}\left[\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{-\frac{V}{1+\gamma}}+s^{-\frac{V}{1+\gamma}}\right]&\mbox{if}\ 1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\leq n\\
=1&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(A.32)

Note that for any
q\geq 1
and
\gamma\neq 1
, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{q}\gamma_{k}\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{q}(1+k)^{-\gamma}\lesssim q^{1-\gamma}\mathbf{1}(\gamma<1)+\mathbf{1}(\gamma>1).

(A.33)

Next, we come to one of the main differences between the current proof and the proof of
Theorem

2.1
, namely the definition of
K_{s}
(see (
A.12
)) which was used for thresholding the increments in the chaining argument in the proof of
Theorem

2.1
. With
\tilde{r}=r\wedge 2
as before, for
0\leq s\leq S-1
, we will redefine

\displaystyle K_{s}:=\begin{cases}(2^{-s+1}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\left(\frac{n}{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s+1}H_{k}}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(1+\gamma)}}&\mbox{if}\ 1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\leq n\\
(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{n}{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}}&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(A.34)

We redefine
\nu_{f}
from (
A.13
) with
K_{s}
chosen as above.

Main proof
. By (
A.14
), we have:

\displaystyle f(x)=\tilde{g}_{f}^{0,L}(x)+\sum_{s=0}^{S}(f(x)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(x))\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(x)=s)+\sum_{s=1}^{S}(\tilde{g}_{f}^{s,L}(x)-\tilde{g}_{f}^{s-1,L}(x))\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(x)\geq s),

where all the notation bears the same meaning. We will use the definition of
\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(\cdot)
from (
4.1
). The definitions of
\Theta_{f,s}^{(1)}
and
\Theta_{f,s}^{(2)}
stay the same as in (
A.15
).
Consequently the following bound holds:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}f(X_{1}))\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tilde{g}_{f}^{0,L}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\tilde{g}_{f}^{0,L}(X_{1}))\bigg|\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{s=0}^{S}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Theta^{(1)}_{f,s}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\Theta^{(1)}_{f,s}(X_{1})\big)\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{s=1}^{S}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(X_{1})\big)\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle=:T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}.

We need to bound the three terms
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
, and
T_{3}
individually. Let us begin with some preliminary estimates.
Note that (
A.32
) implies

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(\sigma)(1+H_{0})}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}+n^{-1/2}D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}.

(A.35)

Now, from (
A.32
) and (
A.34
), we note that whenever
\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(2^{-s}\sigma)=1
, we have
K_{s}=(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{n}{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}}
. Therefore, by combining (
A.32
) with the choice of
K_{s}
in (
A.34
), we have the following for
s\in[S]
,

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;K_{s-1}\frac{\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(2^{-s}\sigma)\left(1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\right)}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle=(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\left(\frac{n}{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\frac{(nr_{n})^{\frac{1}{\gamma+1}}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}+(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+s^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\right\}

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;+(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}}+s^{\frac{V}{2}}\right\}.

(A.36)

In the last inequality above, we have used (
A.30
).
Finally, we will also use the following:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(2^{-s}\sigma)}\gamma_{k}}\,\,\,\,\,\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}}\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}\right)\ \ \mathds{1}_{\gamma<1}+\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}\ \ \mathds{1}_{\gamma>1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\left(\frac{n}{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}\right)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}\right)\ \ \mathds{1}_{\gamma<1,1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\leq n}+\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left(1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\mathds{1}_{\gamma<1,1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\leq n}+\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left(1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(1+\gamma)}}\mathds{1}_{\gamma<1,1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\leq n}+\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+s^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\right\}\mathbf{1}(\gamma<1)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;+\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)}\left[\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}}+s^{\frac{V}{2}}\right].

(A.37)

The first inequality above follows from (
A.33
), the second inequality uses (
A.31
) and the fact that
\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(\lesssim)1
when
n<1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}
. The fourth inequality uses the fact that when
n\geq 1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}
,

n^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left(1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\leq n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left(1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(1+\gamma)}}\,.

The last inequality used the bound on
\sum_{k}H_{k}
, namely (
A.30
).

Bound on
T_{1}
. By
A.3
, we then have:

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i}\left|\tilde{g}_{f}^{0,L}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\tilde{g}_{f}^{0,L}(X)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\inf_{1\leq q\leq n}\left(\sqrt{\sigma^{\tilde{r}}\sum_{k=0}^{q}\gamma_{k}}\sqrt{1+H_{0}}+bq\frac{1+H_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}+b\gamma_{q}\sqrt{n}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sqrt{\sigma^{\tilde{r}}\left(\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(\sigma)^{1-\gamma}\mathbf{1}(\gamma<1)+\mathbf{1}(\gamma>1)\right)}\sqrt{1+H_{0}}+2b\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(\sigma)\frac{1+H_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

The first inequality above uses
A.3
. The second inequality uses (
A.33
) and the choice
q\equiv\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(\sigma)
. Therefore, by using (
A.35
) and (
A.4
), we get:

\boxed{\begin{aligned} T_{1}&\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\sigma^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+\sigma^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}}\\
&\;\;\;+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}+n^{-1/2}D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}.\end{aligned}}

Bound on
T_{2}
. Just as in (
A.2
), it suffices to bound
\{T_{2,j}\}_{j=1,2,3,4}
. We begin with bounding
T_{2,1}
.

With
K_{s}
defined as in (
A.34
), the same calculation as in the proof of
Theorem

2.1
yields:

\displaystyle T_{2,1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{S}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{i})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{i})=s)-\mathbb{E}\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=s)\big)\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{s=1}^{S}\inf_{1\leq q\leq n}\left(\sqrt{(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\tilde{r}}\sum_{k=0}^{q}\gamma_{k}}\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}+K_{s-1}q\frac{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}{\sqrt{n}}+K_{s-1}\gamma_{q}\sqrt{n}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{s=1}^{S}\bigg(\sqrt{(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\tilde{r}}\left(\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(2^{-s}\sigma)\mathbf{1}(\gamma<1)+\mathbf{1}(\gamma>1)\right)}\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}

\displaystyle+2K_{s-1}\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(2^{-s}\sigma)\frac{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)

The first inequality above uses
A.3
. The second inequality uses (
A.33
) and the choice
q\equiv\tilde{q}_{n,\gamma}(2^{-s}\sigma)
. Therefore, by using (
A.4
) and (
A.4
), we get:

\boxed{\begin{aligned} T_{2,1}&\lesssim\sum_{s=1}^{S}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+s^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\right\}\\
&\;\;\;\;+\sum_{s=1}^{S}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}}+s^{\frac{V}{2}}\right\}.\end{aligned}}

We now move on to the bound of
T_{2,2}
. As it only involves the
s=0
-th level of chaining, i.e., a supremum over
{\sf exp}(H_{0})
functions, the bound for
T_{2,2}
will be the exact same as
T_{1}
. In other words, we have:

\boxed{\begin{aligned} T_{2,2}&\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\sigma^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+\sigma^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}}\\
&\;\;\;+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}+n^{-1/2}D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}.\end{aligned}}

Next, we go to the bound on
T_{2,3}
. Our key tool is the following elementary inequality:
\mathbb{E}[|X|\mathbf{1}(|X|\geq a)]\leq a^{-1}\mathbb{E}X^{2}
for
a>0
. Applying this inequality yields:

\displaystyle T_{2,3}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}\big|\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=s)\big)\big|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}\big|\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{1})>K_{s})\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\frac{\mathbb{E}[(\Delta_{f}^{s}(X_{1}))^{2}]}{K_{s}}\leq\sqrt{n}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\frac{(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\tilde{r}}}{K_{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s+1}H_{k}}+n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\left(1+\sum_{k=0}^{s+1}H_{k}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}

By combining the above inequality with (
A.30
), we get:

\boxed{\begin{aligned} T_{2,3}&\lesssim\sum_{s=1}^{S}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+s^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\right\}\\
&\;\;\;\;+\sum_{s=1}^{S}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}}+s^{\frac{V}{2}}\right\}.\end{aligned}}

Finally we provide an upper bound on
T_{2,4}
to complete the task of bounding
T_{2}
:

\displaystyle T_{2,4}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}\big|\Delta_{f}^{S}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=S)\big)\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{n}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\|\Delta_{f}^{S}(X_{1})\mathds{1}(\nu_{f}(X_{1})=S)\big)\|_{\tilde{r},P}

\displaystyle\leq\boxed{\sqrt{n}2^{-S}\sigma}\,.

Now that we have bounded
\{T_{2,j}\}_{j=1,2,3,4}
, we can combine all the terms to bound
T_{2}
. This will yield:

\boxed{\begin{aligned} T_{2}&\lesssim\sum_{s=0}^{S}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+s^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\right\}\\
&\;\;\;\;+\sum_{s=0}^{S}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}}+s^{\frac{V}{2}}\right\}+\sqrt{n}2^{-S}\sigma\\
&\;\;\;\;+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}+n^{-1/2}D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}\end{aligned}}

We now move on to bounding
T_{3}
.

Bound for
T_{3}
.
The initial steps are exactly the same as in the proof of
Theorem

2.1
. In particular,

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigg|\sum_{s=1}^{S}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\Theta^{(2)}_{f,s}(X_{1})\big)\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{s=1}^{S}\inf_{1\leq q\leq n}\left(\sqrt{(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\tilde{r}}\sum_{k=0}^{q}\gamma_{k}}\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}+K_{s-1}q\frac{1+\sum_{k=0}^{s}H_{k}}{\sqrt{n}}+K_{s-1}\gamma_{q}\sqrt{n}\right)\,.

It is evident from the above upper bound on
T_{3}
that it is similar to that of
T_{2,1}
. Therefore we obtain:

\boxed{\begin{aligned} T_{3}&\lesssim\sum_{s=1}^{S}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+s^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\right\}\\
&\;\;\;\;+\sum_{s=1}^{S}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}}+s^{\frac{V}{2}}\right\}.\end{aligned}}

Combining all the bounds obtained so far, we have:

\boxed{\begin{aligned} T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}&\lesssim\sum_{s=0}^{S}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+s^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\right\}\\
&\;\;\;\;+\sum_{s=0}^{S}(2^{-s}\sigma)^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}(2^{s\alpha}\vee s)}\left\{\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}}+s^{\frac{V}{2}}\right\}+\sqrt{n}2^{-S}\sigma\\
&\;\;\;\;+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}+n^{-1/2}D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}\end{aligned}}

Note that we still have a parameter
S\geq 0
to choose. Now that we have obtained bounds from
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
, we now highlight the choice of
S
for different regimes of
\alpha
,
r
, and
\gamma
.

A.4.1
Rates for Donsker class when
0\leq\alpha<\tilde{r}

In this subsection, we consider the case when
0\leq\alpha<2
, which implies
\alpha<\tilde{r}(1+\gamma)/\gamma
. By choosing
S\geq 0
satisfying

2^{S}>n^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}+\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\theta^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}},

combined with the bounds for
T_{1},T_{2}
, and
T_{3}
, we get:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sigma^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+\sigma^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}}

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}+n^{-1/2}D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}

As
D(\theta/\sigma)^{\alpha}(\log{(B/\sigma)})^{V}\lesssim n
, we observe the following inequalities:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}+\frac{1}{2(\gamma+1)}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}+\frac{V}{2(\gamma+1)}}=\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}},

(A.38)

and

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}+\frac{1}{\gamma}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{1+\gamma}+\frac{V}{1+\gamma}}=n^{-1/2}D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}.

(A.39)

Combining the above observations yields a conclusion.

A.4.2
Non-Donsker class: when
\tilde{r}<\alpha<\tilde{r}(1+\gamma)/\gamma

In this subsection, we consider the non-Donsker class when
\tilde{r}<\alpha<\tilde{r}(1+\gamma)/\gamma
. By choosing
S\geq 0
satisfying

2^{S}\sim n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+2-\tilde{r}}}\sigma\left(D\theta^{\alpha}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+2-\tilde{r}}},

combined with the bounds for
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
, and
T_{3}
, we get:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sigma^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\left[D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\right]^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+\left(D\theta^{\alpha}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+2-\tilde{r}}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}}n^{\frac{\alpha-\tilde{r}}{2(\alpha+2-\tilde{r})}}

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}+n^{-1/2}D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;+\left(D\theta^{\alpha}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+2-\tilde{r}}}n^{\frac{\alpha-\tilde{r}}{2(\alpha+2-\tilde{r})}}\left(\log{\big(n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+2-\tilde{r}}}\sigma\theta^{-\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+2-\tilde{r}}}\big)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}}+\sigma^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}}.

As
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+2-\tilde{r}}}
, the last term above is non-negative. The conclusion now follows by combining (
A.4.1
), (
A.4.1
), and noting that

\log{\big(n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+2-\tilde{r}}}\sigma\theta^{-\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+2-\tilde{r}}}\big)}\leq\log{\big(n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+2-\tilde{r}}}\sigma\big)}\leq\log{\big(n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+2-\tilde{r}}}B\big)}\leq\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}.

A.4.3
Non-Donsker class: when
\alpha>\tilde{r}(1+\gamma)/\gamma

We end this proof with the final case when
\alpha>\tilde{r}(1+\gamma)/\gamma
. By choosing
S\geq 0
satisfying

2^{S}\sim n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+\frac{(2-\tilde{r})(\gamma+1)}{\gamma}}}\sigma\left(D\theta^{\alpha}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+\frac{(2-\tilde{r})(\gamma+1)}{\gamma}}},

combined with the bounds for
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
, and
T_{3}
, we get, as above:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{\gamma(\alpha-\tilde{r})+(2-\tilde{r})}{2(\alpha\gamma+(2-\tilde{r})(\gamma+1))}}\left(D\theta^{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{\alpha\gamma+(2-\tilde{r})(\gamma+1)}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+\sigma^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\sqrt{D\left(\frac{\theta}{\alpha}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}}

\displaystyle+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+1}}+n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\sigma^{\frac{\tilde{r}}{2}}\left(D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}

\displaystyle+n^{\frac{\gamma(\alpha-\tilde{r})}{2(\alpha\gamma+(2-\tilde{r})(\gamma+1))}}\left(D\theta^{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{2\gamma+2-\tilde{r}}{2(\alpha\gamma+(2-\tilde{r})(\gamma+1))}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}}+n^{-1/2}D\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\sigma}\right)}\right)^{V}

In the last inequality, we have used the bound
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+(2-\tilde{r})(1+\gamma^{-1})}}
, (
A.4.1
), and (
A.4.1
). This completes the proof.

A.5
Proof of Theorem
5.1

The proof of Theorem
5.1
follows from standard peeling argument. We provide the proof here for completeness.

Note that by definition of
\hat{f}_{n}
, we have:

\hat{f}_{n}\in{\arg\min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{P}_{n}(\ell(Y,f(X)))\,.

Fix
s>1
large enough to be fixed later. For the rest of the proof, define
A_{j}:=\{f:2^{2j}s^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}\leq\mathcal{E}(\hat{f}_{n})\leq 2^{2j+2}s^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}:f\in\mathcal{F}\}
. We then have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}(\hat{f}_{n})>s\delta_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{f:\mathcal{E}(f)>s\delta_{n}}\mathbb{P}_{n}\left(\ell(Y,f_{*}(X))-\ell(Y,f(X))\right)\geq 0\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{f\in A_{j}}\mathbb{P}_{n}\left(\ell(Y,f_{*}(X))-\ell(Y,f(X))\right)\geq 0\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{f\in A_{j}}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\left(\ell(Y,f_{*}(X))-\ell(Y,f(X))\right)\geq\inf_{f\in A_{j}}\mathbb{P}\left(\ell(Y,f(X))-\ell(Y,f_{*}(X))\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\mathcal{E}(f)\leq 2^{2j+2}s^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}}\left|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\left(\ell(Y,f_{*}(X))-\ell(Y,f(X))\right)\right|\right]}{\inf_{f\in A_{j}}\mathcal{E}(f)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\sup_{\lVert f-f_{\star}\rVert_{L_{2}(P_{X})\leq c^{-1/2}2^{j+1}s\delta_{n}}}\big|\mathbb{G}_{n}(g)\big|}{\sqrt{n}2^{2j+1}s^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}}

In the last display, we have used the fact that
\mathcal{E}(f)\geq c\lVert f-f_{\star}\rVert^{2}_{L_{2}(P_{X})}
. Now by the
L
-Lipschitzness of
\ell(\cdot,\cdot)
, we have:

\lVert\ell(Y,f(X))-\ell(Y,f_{\star}(X))\rVert^{2}_{L_{2}(P)}\leq L^{2}\lVert f-f_{\star}\rVert^{2}_{L_{2}(P_{X})}\leq L^{2}c^{-1}2^{2j+2}s^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}.

Now by the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
in
Theorem

5.1
, the above displays imply that

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}(\hat{f}_{n})>s\delta_{n}\right)\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\delta}}\big|\mathbb{G}_{n}(g)\big|}{\sqrt{n}2^{2j+1}s^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}}.

By applying
Theorem

4.1
and abbreviating
\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{G}_{\delta})
as
\Pi_{n}(\delta)
(defined in
Theorem

4.1
), we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}(\hat{f}_{n})>s\delta_{n}\right)\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}2^{2j+1}s^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{Lc^{-1/2}2^{j+1}s\delta_{n}}}\big|\mathbb{G}_{n}(g)\big|\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Pi_{n}\left(Lc^{-\frac{1}{2}}2^{j+1}s\delta_{n}\right)}{\sqrt{n}2^{2j+2}s^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}}.

Choose
s>2\sqrt{c}/L
, then by using the fact that
\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{G}_{\delta})/\delta^{t}
is decreasing for some
t\in(0,2)
and
\Pi_{n}(\delta_{n})\leq\sqrt{n}\delta_{n}^{2}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}(\hat{f}_{n})>s\delta_{n}\right)\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(LC^{-\frac{1}{2}}2^{j+1}s)^{t}\sqrt{n}\delta_{n}^{2}}{\sqrt{n}2^{2j+2}s^{2}\delta_{n}^{2}}\lesssim s^{-(2-t)},

where the
\lesssim
hides constants free of
s
. The moment bound follows by integrating the above tail bound.

B
Proofs of Corollaries from main paper

Proof of
2.1
, Part (1).

With
r>2
, if
\beta>\frac{r}{r-2}
, then note that for
q\geq 1
, by (
2.3
), we get:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(q)\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}u^{-\frac{2}{r}}\,du+\sum_{i=2}^{q+1}\int_{0}^{i^{-\beta}}u^{-\frac{2}{r}}\,du\lesssim 1+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}i^{-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}\lesssim 1,

where all the implicit constants are free of
q
. The above display implies that
\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({\cdot})
can be chosen as a constant function and consequently
2.2
holds. Further note that from (
2.2
), we have:

\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\sigma)\lesssim 1\vee\left(\frac{n}{1+\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({\sigma},\mathcal{F})}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\frac{b\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\sigma)(1+\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({\sigma},\mathcal{F}))}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{b}{\sqrt{n}}(1+\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({\sigma},\mathcal{F}))+bn^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}}(1+\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({\sigma},\mathcal{F}))^{\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{b\sigma^{-\alpha}}{\sqrt{n}}+bn^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}}\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha\beta}{1+\beta}}\lesssim bn^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}}\sigma^{-\frac{\alpha\beta}{1+\beta}},

(B.1)

where we have used
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
in the last inequality. With these observations, we now split the proof into two cases.

When
\alpha<2
. We observe that

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({u},\mathcal{F})}\,du\leq\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}u^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\,du\lesssim\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\sigma.

Here we have used
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
in the above display.
Combining the above observation with
Theorem

2.1
and (
B
) completes the proof in this case.

When
