\alpha>2
. We observe that

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({u},\mathcal{F})}\,du\leq\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}u^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\,du\lesssim\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}.

Therefore, by choosing
a\gtrsim n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
, we get:

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({u},\mathcal{F})}\,du\lesssim\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\lesssim a\frac{n^{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)\left(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}}{n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{4}}}\lesssim a.

Note that
n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\sigma
whenever
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
.
By combining the above observation with
Theorem

2.1
and (
B
) completes the proof in this case.

Proof of Part (2)
. With
r>2
, if
\beta<\frac{r}{r-2}
, then note that for
q\geq 1
, by (
2.3
), we get:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(q)\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}u^{-2/r}\ dr+\sum_{i=2}^{q}\int_{0}^{i^{-\beta}}u^{-2/r}du

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{q}i^{-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}\right)\lesssim q^{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}\,.

Regarding the complexity of
\mathcal{F}
, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0:\ 2^{-k}\sigma\geq u}\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({2^{-k}\sigma},\mathcal{F})\gtrsim\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor\log_{2}{(\sigma/u)}\rfloor}(2^{-k}\sigma)^{-\alpha}\gtrsim u^{-\alpha}.

By (
2.2
), it is now easy to check that

\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(u)\lesssim 1+\left(\frac{n}{1+u^{-\alpha}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}\lesssim 1+(nu^{\alpha})^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},

and

\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(u))\lesssim 1+\left(u^{\alpha}n\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}\left(1-\frac{\beta(r-2)}{r}\right)}=:\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({u})\,.

As
\beta<r/(r-2)
, the function
\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({\cdot})
is non-decreasing in
u
as required in
2.2
. We also have

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\star}_{n}({u})(1+\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({u},\mathcal{F}))

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}{1+\beta}}u^{-\alpha\left(1-\frac{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}{1+\beta}\right)}+u^{-\alpha}

\displaystyle=n^{\frac{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}{1+\beta}}\left(u^{\alpha}\right)^{-\frac{2\beta}{1+\beta}\left(1-\frac{1}{r}\right)}+u^{-\alpha}:=R_{n,\beta}^{(1)}{(u)},

which is non-increasing in
\delta
as
r>2
. This verifies the other requirement for
2.2
. We split the rest of the proof into three parts as follows.

When
\alpha<2
. Observe that

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{R_{n,\beta}^{(1)}(u)}\,du\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}{2(1+\beta)}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha(r-1)\beta}{r(1+\beta)}}+\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\sigma\,.

For the last inequality, we have used
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
. As (
B
) continues to hold, therefore the above display combined with
Theorem

2.1
completes the proof in this case.

When
2<\alpha<\frac{r(1+\beta)}{(r-1)\beta}
. In this case, we note that

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{R_{n,\beta}^{(1)}(u)}\,du\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}{2(1+\beta)}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha(r-1)\beta}{r(1+\beta)}}+\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\,.

By choosing
a\gtrsim n^{\frac{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}{2(1+\beta)}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha(r-1)\beta}{r(1+\beta)}}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{R_{n,\beta}^{(1)}(u)}\,du

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}{2(1+\beta)}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha(r-1)\beta}{r(1+\beta)}}+\left(n^{-\frac{\beta(r-1)}{r(1+\beta)}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha(r-1)\beta}{r(1+\beta)}}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}{2(1+\beta)}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha(r-1)\beta}{r(1+\beta)}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\sigma\,.

For the last two inequalities, we have used
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
. As (
B
) continues to hold, therefore the above display combined with
Theorem

2.1
completes the proof in this case.

When
\alpha>\frac{r(1+\beta)}{(r-1)\beta}
. In this case, we note that

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{R_{n,\beta}^{(1)}(u)}\,du\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}{2(1+\beta)}}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha(r-1)\beta}{r(1+\beta)}}\,.

Consequently, by choosing
a\gtrsim n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\sigma
(as
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
), we observe that

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{R_{n,\beta}^{(1)}(u)}\,du\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}{2(1+\beta)}}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha(r-1)\beta}{r(1+\beta)}}\lesssim an^{\frac{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}{2(1+\beta)}}\big(n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\big)^{-\frac{\alpha\beta(r-1)}{r(1+\beta)}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}=a.

Once again, (
B
) continues to hold. Therefore the above display combined with
Theorem

2.1
completes the proof in this case.
∎

Proof of
3.1
.

Given a function
f\in\mathcal{F}
, let
f_{+}=f\ \vee\ 0
and similarly
f_{-}=(-f)\ \vee\ 0
. Note that
f=f_{+}\ -\ f_{-}
. Also define

\mathcal{F}_{+}:=\{f_{+}:\ f\in\mathcal{F}\},\quad\mathcal{F}_{-}=\{f_{-}:\ f\in\mathcal{F}\}.

Note that

\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|\mathbb{G}_{n}(f)|\leq\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{+}}|\mathbb{G}_{n}(f)|\ +\ \mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{-}}|\mathbb{G}_{n}(f)|.

We will only bound the first term in the above inequality as the other can be bounded in exactly the same way. Recall that
\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\leq\sigma
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
. Two key observations will be used in the rest of the proof, namely

\mathscr{H}_{[\ ]}(\sigma,\mathcal{F}_{+},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})=0=:\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({\sigma},\mathcal{F}),

(B.2)

and

\mathscr{H}_{[\ ]}(\delta,\mathcal{F}_{+},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq\mathscr{H}_{[\ ]}(\delta,\mathcal{F},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq C_{1}\delta^{-\alpha}=:\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({\delta},\mathcal{F})\quad\forall\ \delta<\sigma.

(B.3)

To see (
B.2
), note that
\sup_{f\in{\it cf.}_{+}}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)\leq\sigma
and clearly all
f\in\mathcal{F}_{+}
are non-negative functions. Therefore, the bracket formed by the constant functions
g^{L}(x)\equiv 0
and
g^{U}(x)\equiv\sigma
contains all of
\mathcal{F}_{+}
, thereby establishing (
A.11
).

On the other hand, (
B.3
) follows directly by invoking
Lemma

D.1
, part 4.

Next, we note that, from (
2.2
), we have:

\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\sigma)\lesssim 1\vee\left(\frac{n}{1+\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({\sigma},\mathcal{F})}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\sigma)(1+\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({\sigma},\mathcal{F}))}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{b}{\sqrt{n}}(1+\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({\sigma},\mathcal{F}))+\sigma n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}}(1+\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({\sigma},\mathcal{F}))^{\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}}\lesssim\sigma n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}}.

(B.4)

The last inequality above uses (
A.11
).

Proof of part 1
. When
\beta>1
, note that

\sum_{k=1}^{q}\beta_{k}\lesssim\sum_{k=1}^{q}(1+k)^{-\beta}\lesssim 1,

for
q\geq 1
, where all the implicit constants are free of
q
. The above display implies that
\Lambda_{n,\infty}^{*}(\cdot)
can be chosen as a constant function and consequently
3.1
holds.
Therefore, by combining (
B.4
) with (
B.3
) and
Theorem

3.1
, we get:

\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{+}}|\mathbb{G}_{n}(f)|\leq C\left(\sigma n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}}+\inf\left\{0\leq a\leq 8\sqrt{n}\sigma:\ a\geq C_{0}\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({u},\mathcal{F})}\,du\right\}\right).

(B.5)

We split the next part of the proof into two further cases.

When
\alpha<2
. We observe that

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({u},\mathcal{F})}\,du\leq\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}u^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\,du\lesssim\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\sigma.

Here we have used
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
in the above display.
Combining the above observation with (
B.5
) completes the proof in this case.

When
\alpha>2
. We observe that

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({u},\mathcal{F})}\,du\leq\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}u^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\,du\lesssim\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}.

Therefore, by choosing
a\gtrsim n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
, we get:

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({u},\mathcal{F})}\,du\lesssim\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\lesssim a\frac{n^{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)\left(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}}{n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{4}}}\lesssim a.

Note that
n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\sigma
whenever
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
.
By combining the above observation with (
B.5
) completes the proof in this case.

Proof of Part (2)
. For
\beta<1
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{q}\beta_{k}\lesssim\sum_{k=1}^{q}(1+k)^{-\beta}\lesssim q^{1-\beta}\,.

for
q\geq 1
, where all the implicit constants are free of
q
. By (
B.3
), we can work with
\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({u},\mathcal{F})=Cu^{-\alpha}
for some constant
C>0
.
Regarding the complexity of
\mathcal{F}
, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0:\ 2^{-k}\sigma\geq u}\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({2^{-k}\sigma},\mathcal{F})\gtrsim\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor\log_{2}{(\sigma/u)}\rfloor}(2^{-k}\sigma)^{-\alpha}\gtrsim u^{-\alpha}.

By (
2.2
), it is now easy to check that

\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(u)\lesssim 1+\left(\frac{n}{1+u^{-\alpha}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}\lesssim 1+(nu^{\alpha})^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}},

and

\sum_{k=1}^{\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(u)}\beta_{k}\lesssim 1+\left(u^{\alpha}n\right)^{\frac{1-\beta}{1+\beta}}=:\Lambda_{n,\infty}^{*}(u)\,.

As
\beta<1
, the function
\Lambda_{n,\infty}^{*}(\cdot)
is non-decreasing in
u
as required in
3.1
. We also have

\displaystyle\Lambda_{,\infty}^{*}(u)(1+\mathbb{H}_{\infty}({u},\mathcal{F}))

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta}{1+\beta}}\delta^{-\alpha\left(1-\frac{1-\beta}{1+\beta}\right)}+u^{-\alpha}

\displaystyle=n^{\frac{1-\beta}{1+\beta}}\left(\delta^{\alpha}\right)^{-\frac{2\beta}{1+\beta}}+u^{-\alpha}:=R_{n,\beta}^{(2)}{(u)},

which is non-increasing in
u
. This verifies the other requirement for
3.1
. We split the rest of the proof into three parts as follows.

When
\alpha<2
. Observe that

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{R_{n,\beta}^{(2)}(u)}\,du\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta}{2(1+\beta)}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha\beta}{1+\beta}}+\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\sigma\,.

For the last inequality, we have used
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
. As (
B.4
) continues to hold, therefore the above display combined with
Theorem

3.1
completes the proof in this case.

When
2<\alpha<\frac{1+\beta}{\beta}
. In this case, we note that

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{R_{n,\beta}^{(2)}(u)}\,du\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta}{2(1+\beta)}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha\beta}{1+\beta}}+\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\,.

By choosing
a\gtrsim n^{\frac{1-\beta}{2(1+\beta)}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha\beta}{1+\beta}}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{R_{n,\beta}^{(2)}(u)}\,du

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta}{2(1+\beta)}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha\beta}{1+\beta}}+\left(n^{-\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha\beta}{1+\beta}}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta}{2(1+\beta)}}\sigma^{1-\frac{\alpha\beta}{1+\beta}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\sigma\,.

For the last two inequalities, we have used
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
. As (
B.4
) continues to hold, therefore the above display combined with
Theorem

3.1
completes the proof in this case.

When
\alpha>\frac{1+\beta}{\beta}
. In this case, we note that

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{R_{n,\beta}^{(2)}(u)}\,du\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta\left(1-\frac{2}{r}\right)}{2(1+\beta)}}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha(r-1)\beta}{r(1+\beta)}}\,.

Consequently, by choosing
a\gtrsim n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\sigma
(as
\sigma\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
), we observe that

\int_{\frac{a}{2^{6}\sqrt{n}}}^{\sigma}\sqrt{R_{n,\beta}^{(2)}(u)}\,du\lesssim n^{\frac{1-\beta}{2(1+\beta)}}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha\beta}{1+\beta}}\lesssim an^{\frac{1-\beta}{2(1+\beta)}}\big(n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\big)^{-\frac{\alpha\beta}{1+\beta}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}=a.

Once again, (
B.4
) continues to hold. Therefore the above display combined with
Theorem

3.1
completes the proof in this case.

∎

Proof of
4.1
.

We invoke
Theorem

4.1
with
r=2
,
V=0
,
K_{r}=K\sim 1
,
D=\theta=1
,
\sigma\sim 1
. We will show that each of the four terms in the definition of
\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{F})
in
Theorem

4.1
, part (3), is bounded (up to constants) by
n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
. To wit, observe that the first and the fourth term add up to

\lesssim n^{\frac{\gamma(\alpha-2)}{2\alpha\gamma}}=n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}}.

The second and third terms add up to

\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}}.

Here the last inequality uses the fact that
\alpha\geq 2(1+\gamma)/\gamma
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
4.2
.

The conclusion follows by invoking
Theorem

4.1
with
r=2
,
K_{r}=1
,
D
,
\alpha=0
, and
V,\sigma
as is.
∎

Proof of
5.1
.

Note that by the definition of
\hat{f}_{n}
, we have
\mathbb{P}_{n}(\ell(Y,\hat{f}_{n}(X))-\ell(Y,f_{\star}(X)))\leq 0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\hat{f}_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\left(\ell(Y,\hat{f}_{n}(X))-\ell(Y,f^{\star}(X))\right)+(P-\mathbb{P}_{n})\left(\ell(Y,\hat{f}_{n}(X))-\ell(Y,f^{\star}(X))\right)

\displaystyle\leq(P-\mathbb{P}_{n})\left(\ell(Y,\hat{f}_{n}(X))-\ell(Y,f^{\star}(X))\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)\left(\ell(Y,\hat{f}_{n}(X))-\ell(Y,f^{\star}(X))\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\left|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)g(X,Y)\right|

where
\mathcal{G}
is defined as in (
5.4
),
and
P
is the expectation with respect to the distribution of the new sample
(X,Y)
. Note that all the terms above are functions of
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})
. Therefore, to bound the
expected generalization error
we need to bound the expected supremum of the empirical process. An application of
3.1
with
\sigma\sim 1
completes the proof.

∎

Proof of
5.2
.

By invoking
4.2
, we have

\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{G}_{\delta})=\delta\big(nD^{\gamma}(\log{(B/\sigma)})^{V}\big)^{\frac{1}{2(\gamma+1)}}+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+1}}(D(\log{(B/\sigma)})^{V})^{\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+1}}.

Note that
\Pi_{n}(\delta)/\delta^{t}
is decreasing in
\delta
for any
t\in(1,2)
. next by choosing

\delta_{n}=\left(\frac{D}{n}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}(\log{(Bn)})^{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)},

it is east to check that
\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{G}_{\delta_{n}})\leq\sqrt{n}\delta_{n}^{2}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
5.3
.

As
\alpha>2(1+\gamma^{-1})
, under (
5.6
), by invoking the definition of
\Pi_{n}(\cdot)
from
Theorem

4.1
, part (3), we get:

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{G}_{\delta})

\displaystyle=n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\delta\left[\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\delta}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\delta}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}}\right]+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\delta}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{\gamma+1}}

\displaystyle+n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\delta\left(\log{\left(\frac{B}{\delta}\right)}\right)^{\frac{V\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}.

Once again, we observe that
\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{G}_{\delta})/\delta^{t}
is decreasing for all
t\in(1,2)
. Therefore, by choosing

\delta_{n}=n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\left(\log{(Bn)}\right)^{\frac{V}{2}},

we observe that
\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{G}_{\delta_{n}})\leq\sqrt{n}\delta_{n}^{2}
. By invoking
Theorem

5.1
, we complete the proof.
∎

Proof of
5.4
.

The proof of this proceeds using a technique from
[
66
, Theorem 3.6]
. Observe that by writing expectations of non-negative functions as tail integrals, given any
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we have:

\displaystyle|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)f|

\displaystyle\leq|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)f_{+}|+|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)f_{-}|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}_{n}}(f_{+}(X)>t)-\mathbb{P}_{P}(f_{+}(X)>t)\right)\,dt\bigg|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\bigg|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}_{n}}(f_{-}(X)>t)-\mathbb{P}_{P}(f_{-}(X)>t)\right)\,dt\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 2b\sup_{C\in\mathcal{C}}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)(C)|,

where we have used the fact that all the level sets of
f_{+}
and
f_{-}
are contained in
\mathcal{C}
. Note that

\sup_{C\in\mathcal{C}}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)(C)|=\sup_{f\in\{\mathbf{1}(C):\ C\in\mathcal{C}\}}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)(f)|.

The condition (
5.8
) gives the
L_{1}
entropy of the class of functions
\{\mathbf{1}(C):\ C\in\mathcal{C}\}
. Invoking
Theorem

4.1
with
r=1
completes the proof.
∎

C
Proofs from Applications

This section presents the proofs of all our results from
Section

6
sequentially.

C.1
Proof of Theorem
6.1

We begin with a brief discussion on neural net architecture for the sake of completion. For ease of exposition, we will stick to the ReLU activation function and a feedforward fully connected neural network (also called multi-layer perceptron). Such neural networks have attracted a lot of attention due to their empirical success. We also recall the notation
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NN}}(d_{\mathrm{in}},d_{\mathrm{out}},N,L,B)
where
d_{\mathrm{in}},d_{\mathrm{out}}
are the input and the output dimensions,
N
and
L
denote the width and the depth, while
B
is the bound on the weights. Functions belonging to a ReLU based neural network take the following form:

f(x)=\mathcal{L}_{L}\ \circ\ \sigma\ \circ\ \mathcal{L}_{L-1}\ \circ\ \sigma\ \circ\ \cdots\mathcal{L}_{1}\ \circ\ \sigma\ \circ\ \mathcal{L}_{0}(x),

where
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}(x):=W_{\ell}x+b_{\ell}
for
W_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\ell}\times d_{\ell-1}}
,
b_{\ell}\in d_{\ell}
,
d_{0}=d
,
d_{L}=1
and
\sigma
is applied coordinatewise. Here
L
denotes the number of layers and
(d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{L})
is typically called a width vector with the width
W=\max_{\ell=0}^{L}d_{\ell}
. The bound on the weights implies
\lVert W_{\ell}\rVert_{\infty}\leq b
and
\lVert b_{\ell}\rVert_{\infty}\leq b
for
\ell=0,1,\ldots,L
. Following
[
48
, Definition 2]
, the truncated class
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{NN}}
is defined as follows:

\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{NN}}(d_{\mathrm{in}},d_{\mathrm{out}},N,L,B):=\{\tilde{f}(x)=\mathrm{sign}(f(x))(|f(x)|\wedge(2b)):\,f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NN}}(d_{\mathrm{in}},d_{\mathrm{out}},N,L,B)\}.

Note that as we are working with a fully connected network, we have suppressed
W
, the number of active weights, throughout the notation used.

As in general sieve-based estimation procedure for nonparametric regression function, the proof of Theorem
6.1
consists of two key ingredients: bounding bias/approximation error and bounding variance/statistical error. We will need a preparatory result each for the above two components.

To quantify the bias/approximation error of a Hölder smooth function by neural nets, we use the following result. It is a simple modification of
[
48
, Theorem 4]
.

Proposition C.1
.

There exists universal constants
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
c_{3}
,
N_{0}
depending only on
d
,
s
,
C
,
b
such that given any
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
f\in\Sigma(s,C)
with
\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\leq b
and any arbitrary
N\geq N_{0}
, it holds that

\inf_{\phi\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{NN}}(d,1,N,c_{1},N^{c_{2}})}\|f-\phi\|_{\infty}\leq c_{3}N^{-\frac{2s}{d}}\,,

(C.1)

Concerning the sampling variance, we need a bound on the complexity of the collection of neural networks. Towards that end, a bound on the
L_{\infty}
bracketing number of neural networks can also be found in the literature; which we state here in the following proposition, which is taken from
[
131
, Lemma 5]
.

Proposition C.2
.

With
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{NN}}(d,1,N,c_{1},N^{c_{2}})
defined above, there exists some constants
c_{4}
,
c_{5}
,
c_{6}
depending only on
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
such that

\mathscr{H}_{[\,]}(\delta,\mathcal{F},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty})\leq c_{4}N^{2}\log{\left(\frac{2c_{5}N^{c_{6}}}{\delta}\right)}\,.

(C.2)

Note that
[
131
, Lemma 5]
provides bounds on the covering number, instead of the bracketing number. However, with respect to the uniform norm, the bracketing number and covering number are equivalent. Hence the conclusion follows.

Completing the proof of
Theorem

6.1
.

Henceforth, denote by
\mathcal{F}_{n}:=\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{NN}}(d,1,N,c_{1},N^{c_{2}})
, where we will later choose
N
depending on
n
. Given
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, define the
best approximator

f_{n}
as:

f_{n}={\arg\min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\mathbb{E}[(f_{*}(X)-f_{n}(X))^{2}]\,.

By (
C.1
), we have
\|f_{n}-f_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\leq c_{3}^{2}N^{-\frac{4s}{d}}
. Recall that
\hat{f}_{n}
is the least-square estimator over
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, i.e.,

\hat{f}_{n}={\arg\min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-f(X_{i}))^{2}\,.

We will prove the result based on the following proposition, which is a slightly adapted version of
Theorem

5.1
.

Proposition C.3
.

Suppose the functions
\phi_{n,1}(\cdot)
and
\phi_{n,2}(\cdot)
satisfy the following inequalities:

\phi_{n,1}(\delta)\geq\sqrt{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\|f-f_{n}\|_{2}\leq\delta}\left|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})(f-f_{n})^{2}\right|\right]

\phi_{n,2}(\delta)\geq\sqrt{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\|f-f_{n}\|_{2}\leq\delta}\left|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\xi(f-f_{n})\right|\right]

Also assume there exists
\alpha\in(0,2)
such that both
\phi_{n,1}(\delta)/\delta^{\alpha}
and
\phi_{n,2}(\delta)/\delta^{\alpha}
are decreasing functions. Finally let
r_{n}
be a sequence satisfying

\displaystyle r_{n}^{2}\left(\phi_{n,1}(r_{n}^{-1})+\phi_{n,2}(r_{n}^{-1})\right)\leq\sqrt{n}\,,

(C.3)

\displaystyle r_{n}\|f_{n}-f_{0}\|_{2}=r_{n}\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\|f-f_{0}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,.

(C.4)

Then there exists a constant
C>0
, depending on
\alpha
, such that for any
t>1
, the following holds:

\mathbb{P}(r_{n}\lVert\hat{f}_{n}-f_{\star}\rVert_{2}\geq t)\leq Ct^{2-\alpha}.

The proof of this proposition requires a slight modification of the peeling argument used in the proof of
Theorem

5.1
. We will first complete the proof of
Theorem

6.1
assuming the above claim. We will then prove the proposition itself.

To obtain bounds on
\phi_{n,1},\phi_{n,2}
we use Theorem
4.1
. For
\phi_{n,1}
we consider the function class
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{2}=\{(f-f_{n})^{2}:f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}\}
. From
Lemma

D.1
, part 1, we have:

\mathscr{H}_{[\,]}(\delta,\mathcal{F}_{n}^{2},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty})\leq 2\mathscr{H}_{[\,]}(\delta/8b,\mathcal{F}_{n},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty})\leq c_{4}N^{2}\log{\left(\frac{16bc_{5}N^{c_{6}}}{\delta}\right)}\,.

Applying part 1. of Theorem
4.1
(with
\alpha=0
and
\tilde{r}=2
) and (
C.2
), we obtain (ignoring the constants):

\phi_{n,1}(\delta)\lesssim\ \delta n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left(N^{2}\log{\left(\frac{N}{\delta}\right)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(N^{2}\log{\left(\frac{N}{\delta}\right)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\,.

(C.5)

The analysis for
\phi_{n,2}
is similar; as
f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
is bounded by
2b
and
\xi
is bounded by
b_{e}
, the functions
\xi(f-f_{n})
is bounded by
4bb_{e}
. Call this collection to be
\mathcal{F}_{n,\xi}
. By Lemma
D.1
we have:

\mathscr{H}_{[\,]}(\delta,\mathcal{F}_{n,\xi},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty})\leq\mathscr{H}_{[\,]}(\delta/b_{e},\mathcal{F}_{n},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty})\,.

(C.6)

Therefore, another application of part 1. of Theorem
4.1
(with
\alpha=0,\tilde{r}=2
), coupled with (
C.2
), we get:

\phi_{n,2}(\delta)\lesssim\ \delta n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left(N^{2}\log{\left(\frac{N}{\delta}\right)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(N^{2}\log{\left(\frac{N}{\delta}\right)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\,.

(C.7)

To satisfy (
C.3
) and (
C.4
), some simple algebra yields that the rate
r_{n}
satisfies:

\displaystyle r_{n}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{\frac{2s}{d}}

(C.8)

\displaystyle r_{n}\left(\log{(Nr_{n})}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{n}{N^{2}}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\,.

(C.9)

Taking the width
N
as:

N\sim\left(\frac{n}{\log{n}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{1+\frac{2s}{d}\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)}}

we claim that

r_{n}\sim\left(\frac{n}{\log{n}}\right)^{\frac{s}{d+2s\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)}}

satisfies (
C.8
) and (
C.9
). The first one, i.e. (
C.8
) is immediate from the choice of
N
. For (
C.9
), observe that, our choice of
N
yields:

\left(\frac{n}{N^{2}}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\sim n^{\frac{s}{d+2s\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)}}\left(\log{n}\right)^{\frac{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}{1+\frac{2s}{d}\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)}}\,.

Now, we have:

\displaystyle r_{n}\left(\log{(Nr_{n})}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{n}{\log{n}}\right)^{\frac{s}{d+2s\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)}}\left(\log{\left(\frac{n}{\log{n}}\right)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(1+\gamma)}}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{s}{d+2s\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)}}(\log{n})^{\frac{\gamma}{2(1+\gamma)}-\frac{s}{d+2s\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)}}

\displaystyle=n^{\frac{s}{d+2s\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)}}(\log{n})^{\frac{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}{1+\frac{2s}{d}\left(\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\right)}}\sim\left(\frac{n}{N^{2}}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\,.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
C.3
.

Finally, we present a peeling argument to prove
C.3
. For notational simplicity, define
\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)=(1/n)\sum_{i}(Y_{i}-f(X_{i}))^{2}
to be the empirical risk and
\mathcal{R}(f)=\mathbb{E}[(Y-f(X))^{2}]
to be the population risk. Define
A_{j}:=\{f:2^{j}tr_{n}^{-1}\leq\lVert f-f_{\star}\rVert_{2}\leq 2^{j+1}tr_{n}^{-1}:f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}\}
. Then, for all
t>1
, we have:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\mathbb{P}\left(r_{n}\|f_{*}-\hat{f}\|_{2}\geq t\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}\\
r_{n}\|f_{*}-\hat{f}\|_{2}\geq t\end{subarray}}\mathcal{R}_{n}(f_{n})-\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)\geq 0\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\phi\in A_{j}}\mathcal{R}_{n}(f_{n})-\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)\geq 0\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\phi\in A_{j}}(\mathcal{R}_{n}(f_{n})-\mathcal{R}(f_{n}))-(\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)-\mathcal{R}(f))\geq\inf_{\phi\in A_{j}}\mathcal{R}(f)-\mathcal{R}(f_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\phi\in A_{j}}\left|(\mathcal{R}_{n}(f_{n})-\mathcal{R}(f_{n}))-(\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)-\mathcal{R}(f))\right|\right]}{\inf_{\phi\in A_{j}}\mathcal{R}(f)-\mathcal{R}(f_{n})}

(C.10)

We bound the numerator and denominator separately. For the denominator note that:

\displaystyle\inf_{f\in A_{j}}\mathcal{R}(f)-\mathcal{R}(f_{n})

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{f:\|f-f_{*}\|_{2}\geq 2^{j-1}tr_{n}^{-1}}\mathcal{R}(f)-\mathcal{R}(f_{*})+\mathcal{R}(f_{*})-\mathcal{R}(f_{n})

\displaystyle\geq 2^{2j-2}t^{2}r_{n}^{-2}-\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\|f-f_{*}\|^{2}\triangleq 2^{2j-2}t^{2}r_{n}^{-2}-\omega^{2}_{n}\,.

For the numerator:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{f\in A_{j}}\left|(\mathcal{R}_{n}(f_{n})-\mathcal{R}(f_{n}))-(\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)-\mathcal{R}(f))\right|\right]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{f\in A_{j}}\left|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)(f-f_{n})^{2}+(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P){\epsilon}(f-f_{n})\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\phi_{n,1}\left(2^{j}tr_{n}^{-1}\right)+\phi_{n,2}\left(2^{j}tr_{n}^{-1}\right)}{\sqrt{n}}\,.

Putting this bound in equation (
C.10
) we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(r_{n}\|\hat{f}-f_{\star}\|_{2}\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\phi_{n,1}\left(2^{j}tr_{n}^{-1}\right)+\phi_{n,2}\left(2^{j}tr_{n}^{-1}\right)}{\sqrt{n}\left(2^{2j-2}t^{2}r_{n}^{-2}-\omega_{n}^{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{r_{n}^{2}\phi_{n,1}\left(2^{j}tr_{n}^{-1}\right)+r_{n}^{2}\phi_{n,2}\left(2^{j}tr_{n}^{-1}\right)}{\sqrt{n}\left(2^{2j-2}t^{2}-r_{n}^{2}\omega_{n}^{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(2^{j}t)^{\alpha}\left(r_{n}^{2}\phi_{n,1}\left(r_{n}^{-1}\right)+r_{n}^{2}\phi_{n,2}\left(r_{n}^{-1}\right)\right)}{\sqrt{n}\left(2^{2j-2}t^{2}-r_{n}^{2}\omega_{n}^{2}\right)}\hskip 7.22743pt[\because\phi_{n}(t)/t^{\alpha}\text{ is decreasing and }2^{j}t\geq 1]

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(2^{j}t)^{\alpha}}{\left(2^{2j-2}t^{2}-r_{n}^{2}\omega_{n}^{2}\right)}\hskip 7.22743pt[\because r_{n}^{2}\phi_{n,1}(r_{n}^{-1})\vee r_{n}^{2}\phi_{n,2}(r_{n}^{-1})\leq\sqrt{n},\text{ equation }\eqref{eq:roc_dnn_1}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(2^{j}t)^{\alpha}}{\left(2^{2j-3}t^{2}\right)}\hskip 7.22743pt[\because r_{n}^{2}w_{n}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\leq 2^{2j-3}t^{2},\text{ equation }\eqref{eq:roc_dnn_2}]

\displaystyle\leq 16t^{2-\alpha}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j(2-\alpha)}\leq Ct^{2-\alpha}\,.

This completes the proof.
∎

C.2
Proof of Theorem
6.2

We prove Theorem
6.2
in this subsection. Using a DNN-based estimator, our proof is essentially similar to that of Theorem
6.1
. For brevity, we highlight the key differences here. Recall the definition of the estimator in (
6.1
) and the collection
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DNN}}
of DNN. First, we bound the approximation error based on the analysis of
[
12
]
. We prove the following lemma:

Lemma C.1
.

There exists universal constants
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
c_{3}
,
N_{0}
depending only on
s
,
C
,
b
such that given any
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{add}}([0,1]^{d},d,b,s)
with
\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\leq b
, and any arbitrary
N\geq N_{0}
, it holds that

\inf_{\phi\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{NN},\mathrm{add}}(d,1,N,c_{1},N^{c_{2}})}\|f-\phi\|_{2}\leq c_{3}\sqrt{d}N^{-2s}\,,

(C.11)

Proof.

To prove the above lemma we use (
C.11
) for each of the univariate components. In particular let
\tilde{\phi}_{j}\in
with depth
c_{1}
, width
N
, and weights bounded by
N^{c_{2}}
such that:

\|\tilde{\phi}_{j}-f_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq CN^{-2s}\,.

Note that changing
\tilde{\phi}_{j}
by
\tilde{\phi}_{j}-\int_{[0,1]}\tilde{\phi}_{j}(z)\,dz
only amounts to altering the bias of the last layer of the neural network, without changing its architecture. Further, as
\int f(z)\,dz=0
, we have:

\bigg\lVert\tilde{\phi}_{j}-\int_{[0,1]}\tilde{\phi}_{j}(z)\,dz-f_{j}\bigg\rVert_{\infty}\leq 2\lVert\tilde{\phi}_{j}-f_{j}\rVert_{\infty}\leq c_{3}N^{-2s},

by changing
c_{3}
appropriately.
Therefore, we can assume without loss of generality that
\int_{[0,1]}\tilde{\phi}_{j}(z)\,dz=0
.
Now consider
\tilde{\phi}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\tilde{\phi}_{j}(x_{j})
for
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})
. Then
\phi\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NN},\mathrm{add}}(d,1,N,c_{1},N^{c_{2}})
.
Furthermore, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(f(X)-\phi(X))^{2}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\int(f(x)-\tilde{\phi}(x))^{2}\,dx=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int(\tilde{\phi}_{j}(x_{j})-f(x_{j}))^{2}\,dx\,\leq c_{3}dN^{-4s},

by changing
c_{3}
as necessary. The conclusion now follows by changing
\phi(x)
to
\mathrm{sign}(\phi(x))(|\phi(x)|\wedge(2b))
and noting that
\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\leq b
, and

\int\big(\mathrm{sign}(\phi(x))(|\phi(x)|\wedge(2b))-f(x)\big)^{2}\,dx\leq\int(f(x)-\tilde{\phi}(x))^{2}\,dx.

∎

To bound the variance/stochastic error, we follow a similar approach as that of the proof of Theorem
6.1
. In particular, thanks to
C.3
, we aim to find
\phi_{n,1}(\delta)
and
\phi_{n,2}(\delta)
following (
C.3
) and (
C.3
).
Recall that we use the collection of neural networks
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NN},\mathrm{add}}(d,1,N,c_{1},N^{c_{2}})
.
Therefore, using (
C.2
) we obtain:

\mathscr{H}_{[\ ]}(\delta,\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DNN}},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim dN^{2}\log{\left(\frac{dN}{\delta}\right)}\,.

The rest of the analysis is similar to that of the proof of Theorem
6.1
, where we use part (1) of Theorem
4.1
to bound
\phi_{n,1}
and
\phi_{n,2}
. To bound
\phi_{n,1}
, we first note that:

\mathscr{H}_{[\ ]}(\delta,\mathcal{F}_{n}^{2},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq 2\mathscr{H}_{[\ ]}(\delta/8b,\mathcal{F}_{n},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim dN^{2}\log{\left(\frac{dN}{\delta}\right)}\,.

Noting that
\phi_{n,1}(\delta)\geq\mathbb{E}[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{2}}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)g|]
we conclude via part (1) of Theorem 4.1 (with
\alpha=0
and
\tilde{r}=2
):

\phi_{n,1}(\delta)\lesssim\ \delta n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left(dN^{2}\log{\left(\frac{N}{\delta}\right)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(dN^{2}\log{\left(\frac{N}{\delta}\right)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\,.

(C.12)

Similarly, for
\phi_{n,2}
, using (
C.6
) and again with part (1) of Theorem
4.1
(with
\alpha=0
and
\tilde{r}=2
) we conclude:

\phi_{n,2}(\delta)\lesssim\ \delta n^{\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}}\left(dN^{2}\log{\left(\frac{N}{\delta}\right)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\left(dN^{2}\log{\left(\frac{N}{\delta}\right)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\,.

(C.13)

Finally, to obtain the rate of convergence, we choose
r_{n}
by solving (
C.3
) and (
C.4
) as follows:

r_{n}\sim\min\left\{\frac{N^{2s}}{\sqrt{d}},\ \left(\frac{n}{dN^{2}(\log{(N)})}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\right\},

where

N\sim n^{\frac{1}{2(1+2s(1+\gamma^{-1}))}}\ d^{\frac{1}{2(2s(1+\gamma)+\gamma)}}.

This completes the proof.

C.3
Proof of
Theorem

6.3

Let us elaborate first on how to extend the BCLS estimator beyond the design points. This discussion is a combination of
[
68
]
and
[
133
]
. An alternate way to formulate the least squares problem in
Section

6.3
goes as follows:

\min_{\{y_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\},\{g_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-y_{i})^{2},

subject to

\displaystyle y_{j}\geq y_{i}+g_{i}^{\top}(X_{j}-X_{i}),

\displaystyle y_{i}\leq 1,\quad y_{i}\geq-1,\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{for all}\ i,j=1,2,\ldots,n,

\displaystyle 1\geq y_{i}+g_{i}^{\top}(v-X_{i}),\,\mbox{for all}\ v\in\partial\Omega.

The existence of unique solutions in the above formulation along with the optimality conditions is derived in
[
133
, Lemmas 2.3 and 2.4]
; also see
[
68
, Equations 3.2—3.4]
. With a notational abuse, let us call the optimizer from the above formulation as
\{y_{i}\}
and
\{g_{i}\}
. We then define
\hat{f}_{n}
on the whole of
\Omega
as follows:

\hat{f}_{n}(x):=\max_{i=1,2,\ldots,n}(y_{i}+\hat{g}_{i}^{\top}(x-X_{i})).

We note that with the above extension
\hat{f}_{n}(X_{i})=\hat{y}_{i}
, i.e., the extension preserves the functions at the sample points. This follows immediately from the first constraint above. As a result, the following inequality continues to hold

\mathbb{P}_{n}(\ell(Y,\hat{f}_{n}(X)))\leq\mathbb{P}_{n}(\ell(Y,f_{\star}(X))).

The above is the only inequality we need to invoke
Theorem

5.1
.

Another useful result that we will need from the literature is a bound on the bracketing entropy of the class of convex functions supported on a compact polytope. This is borrowed from
[
93
, Theorem 4.4]
. We reproduce their result below for completeness.

Proposition C.4
.

Let
\Omega
be a convex body in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with bounded volume. Let
f_{0}
be a convex function in
\Omega
that is bounded by
1
. Also recall that
\mathcal{F}\equiv\mathcal{F}(\Omega)
is the space of convex functions on
\Omega
bounded by
1
. For a fixed
1\leq r<\infty
and
t>0
, let

B_{r}(f_{0};t;\Omega):=\left\{f\in\mathcal{F}:\ \int|f(x)-f_{0}(x)|^{r}\,dx\leq t^{r}\right\}.

Suppose
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{k}\subseteq\Omega
are
d
-simplices with disjoint interiors such that
\cup_{j=1}^{k}\Delta_{j}=\Omega
and
f_{0}
is affine on each
\Delta_{i}
. Then for every
0<u<1
and
t>0
, we have

\mathscr{H}_{[\,]}(u,B_{r}(f_{0};t;\Omega),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{r})\leq C_{d,r}k\left(\log{\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)}\right)^{d+1}\left(\frac{t}{u}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}

for a constant
C_{d,r}
depending only on
r
,
d
, and the volume of
\Omega
.

We are now in position to prove
Theorem

6.3
.

Proof of part (a)
. Let us split the proof into two cases
\beta<1
and
\beta>1
.

\beta<1
case
. As
\beta>2/(d-2)
with
d>4
, there exists
r
large enough such that

d>2r(1+\beta)/((r-1)\beta).

This is possible because in this regime
d\beta>2(1+\beta)
which implies that there exists
r>2
such that

r-1>\frac{2(1+\beta)}{\beta(d-2)-2}.

Such a choice of
r
satisfies the first inequality above. Further as
\beta<1
, it automatically satisfies
\beta<r/(r-2)
.

Now, just as in the proof of
Theorem

6.1
, it suffices to bound the local complexity of the two terms

\phi_{n,1}(\delta)\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\|f-f_{\star}\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta}\left|\mathbb{G}_{n}(f-f_{\star})^{2}\right|\right],

(C.14)

and

\phi_{n,2}(\delta)\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\|f-f_{\star}\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta}\left|\mathbb{G}_{n}(\xi(f-f_{\star}))\right|\right].

(C.15)

For obtaining
\phi_{n,1}(\sigma)
, we need to consider the function class
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}^{2}:=\{(f-f_{n})^{2}:f\in\mathcal{F}_{\delta}\}
where
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}:=\{f:\mathcal{F}:\ \lVert f-f_{\star}\rVert_{L_{2}}\leq\delta\}
. From
Lemma

D.1
, part 1, and
C.4
with
f_{0}=0
,
\Delta_{1}=\Omega
, we have:

\mathscr{H}_{[\,]}(u,\mathcal{F}_{\delta}^{2},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{r})\lesssim C_{d,r}\left(\log{\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)}\right)^{d+1}u^{-\frac{d}{2}}\,,

where the
\lesssim
hides constants depending on
d
,
r
, and the volume of
\Omega
.

Therefore, by invoking
2.1
, part 2, we have:

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\|f-f_{\star}\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta}\left|\mathbb{G}_{n}(f-f_{\star})^{2}\right|\right]\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{d}}\left(\log(n)\right)^{d+1}=:\phi_{n,1}(\delta).

We note that
2.1
does not take into account logarithmic factors in the entropy bound but that can be incorporated to get the above bound with minor technical modifications. We leave the details to the reader.

Moving on to
\phi_{n,2}(\cdot)
, we consider the function space
\mathcal{F}_{\delta,\xi}:=\{\xi(f(x)-f_{\star}(x)):\ f\in\mathcal{F}_{\delta}\}
. By
Lemma

D.1
, part 3, and
C.4
with
f_{0}=0
,
\Delta_{1}=\Omega
, we have:

\mathscr{H}_{[\,]}(u,\mathcal{F}_{\delta,\xi},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{r})\lesssim C_{d,r}\left(\log{\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)}\right)^{d+1}u^{-\frac{d}{2}}\,.

The rest of the argument is same as that for
\phi_{n,1}(\cdot)
. Therefore, we again get:

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\|f-f_{\star}\|_{L_{2}}\leq\delta}\left|\mathbb{G}_{n}(\xi(f-f_{\star}))\right|\right]\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{d}}\left(\log(n)\right)^{d+1}=:\phi_{n,2}(\delta).

Therefore
(\phi_{n,1}+\phi_{n,2})(\delta)
is constant in
\delta
and therefore both
\phi_{n,1}(\delta)/\delta^{t}
and
\phi_{n,2}(\delta)/\delta^{t}
are decreasing for
t\in(0,2)
. The conclusion follows by invoking
Theorem

5.1
.

\beta>1
case
. The proof is similar as in the
\beta<1
case, except in the choice of
r
. Note that as
\beta>1
, there exists
r
such that
\beta>r/(r-2)
. As
d>4
, we are now in the regime of
2.1
, part 1, instead pf
2.1
, part 2. The rest of the computation is the same.

Proof of part (b)
. The conclusion follows from
5.3
choosing
\alpha=d/2
. Once again
5.3
does not take into account logarithmic factors in the entropy but that can be handled with minor technical adjustments which we leave to the reader.

C.4
Proofs of
Theorem

6.4

We begin with some preliminary notation. Given
\mu
with Lebesgue density
f_{\mu}
, its Fisher information is denoted by

I_{\mu}:=\int\lVert\nabla\log{f_{\mu}(x)}\rVert^{2}f_{\mu}(x)\,dx.

Note that by the conditions imposed in
Theorem

6.4
, part (ii), we have:

I_{\mu}<\infty\quad,\quad I_{\nu}<\infty.

(C.16)

Next, we recall the Brenier-McCann polar factorization theorem from
[
23
,
107
]
.

Proposition C.5
.

Suppose
\mu
and
\nu
are absolutely continuous probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then there exists a
\mu
almost everywhere unique map
T_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
which is the gradient of a
d
-variate real-valued convex function, such that
T_{0}\#\mu=\nu
, i.e.,
T_{0}(X)\sim\nu
if
X\sim\mu
.

With
T_{0}
as in
C.5
, let
\rho_{t}
denote the probability density of the measure
((1-t)\mathrm{Id}+tT_{0})\#\mu
. The Fisher information of the Wasserstein geodesic between
\mu
and
\nu
is then defined as

I_{\mu,\nu}:=\int_{t\in[0,1]}\int_{x}\lVert\nabla\log\rho_{t}(x)\rVert^{2}\rho_{t}(x)\,dx\,dt.

Under the assumptions of
Theorem

6.4
, we then have:

I_{\mu,\nu}<\infty.

(C.17)

The following proposition taken from
[
30
]
which will be useful in the sequel.

Proposition C.6
.

Assume
\mu
,
\nu
are compactly supported, absolutely continuous distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Also suppose that (
C.16
) and (
C.17
) hold. Then there exists a constant
C_{\mu,\nu}
depending on
I_{\mu}
,
I_{\nu}
, and
I_{\mu,\nu}
, such that

\bigg|\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}(\mu,\nu)-\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\bigg|\leq C_{\mu,\nu}\varepsilon^{2}.

The next proposition (see
[
130
, Theorem 1.42]
) provides the dual representation of the
2
-Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)
.

Proposition C.7
.

Consider
\mu
,
\nu
which are compactly supported on subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, say
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
respectively (note that absolute continuity is not necessary). Then, the following holds:

\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)=\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert x\rVert^{2}\,d\mu(x)+\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert y\rVert^{2}\,d\nu(y)-\min_{f\in\mathfrak{f}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mu,\nu}(f),

where

\mathfrak{S}_{\mu,\nu}(f):=\int f(x)\,d\mu(x)+\int f^{*}(y)\,d\nu(y),\quad f^{*}(y):=\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}(\langle x,y\rangle-f(x)),\ y\in\mathcal{Y},

and
\mathfrak{f}
denotes the class of convex functions
f
on
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.4
, part (I).

The proof proceeds begin with similar steps as the proofs of
[
30
, Lemmas 3 and 4]
,
[
110
, Prop 2]
. Finally of course we will need to use the maximal inequalities developed here, in particular
Theorem

3.1
.

Without loss of generality, let us assume that both
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are contained in the unit ball of diameter
R\in(0,\infty)
. Also note that
\mathfrak{S}_{\mu,\nu}(f)=\mathfrak{S}_{\mu,\nu}(f+c)
for any constant
c
. Therefore, we can invoke
[
35
, Step II in the proof of Theorem 2.2]
and further replace
\mathfrak{f}
in
C.7
with
\mathfrak{f}_{R}:=\{f\in\mathfrak{f}:\ \lVert f\rVert_{\infty}\leq C_{R}\}
, for some fixed constant
C_{R}>0
.

Now given any
y_{0}\in\mathcal{Y}
,
f\in\mathfrak{f}_{R}
, choose
x_{0}
such that
f^{*}(y_{0})=\langle x_{0},y_{0}\rangle-f(x_{0})
. Given any other
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, note that
f^{*}(y)\geq\langle x_{0},y\rangle-f(x_{0})
. Therefore,

f^{*}(y_{0})-f^{*}(y)\leq\langle x_{0},y_{0}-y\rangle\leq R\lVert y_{0}-y\rVert.

By reversing the roles of
y_{0}
and
y
, we can now conclude that
f^{*}
is
R
-Lipschitz. As
(f^{*})^{*}=f
(by the convexity of
f
), by reversing the roles of
f
and
f^{*}
, we again have that
f
is
R
-Lipschitz. For the sequel, let us write

\mathcal{F}_{R}
to denote the class of convex functions which are
R
-Lipschitz, uniformly bounded by
C_{R}
, and supported inside the unit ball of diameter
R
. The above argument shows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert x\rVert^{2}\,d\mu(x)+\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert y\rVert^{2}\,d\nu(y)-\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{R}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mu,\nu}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert x\rVert^{2}\,d\mu(x)+\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert y\rVert^{2}\,d\nu(y)-\mathfrak{S}_{\mu,\nu}(f_{0}),

where
f_{0}\in{\arg\min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{R}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mu,\nu}(f)
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert x\rVert^{2}\,d\hat{\mu}_{n}(x)+\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert y\rVert^{2}\,d\hat{\nu}_{n}(y)-\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{R}}\mathfrak{S}_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n}}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert x\rVert^{2}\,d\hat{\mu}_{n}(x)+\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert y\rVert^{2}\,d\hat{\nu}_{n}(y)-\mathfrak{S}_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n}}(\hat{f}_{n}),

where
\hat{f}_{n}\in{\arg\min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{R}}\mathfrak{S}_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n}}(f)
. By the optimality of
\hat{f}_{n}
, we then get:

\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})\geq\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert x\rVert^{2}\,d\hat{\mu}_{n}(x)+\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert y\rVert^{2}\,d\hat{\nu}_{n}(y)-\mathfrak{S}_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n}}(f_{0}).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)-\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert x\rVert^{2}\,d(\hat{\mu}_{n}-\mu)(x)\bigg|+\bigg|\frac{1}{2}\int\lVert y\rVert^{2}\,d(\hat{\nu}_{n}-\nu)(y)\bigg|+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{R}}\bigg|\int f\,d(\hat{\mu}_{n}-\mu)\bigg|+\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{R}}\bigg|\int g\,d(\hat{\nu}_{n}-\nu)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{R}}\bigg|\int f\,d(\hat{\mu}_{n}-\mu)\bigg|+2\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{R}}\bigg|\int g\,d(\hat{\nu}_{n}-\nu)\bigg|.

(C.18)

Next by using the optimality of
f_{0}
instead of
\hat{f}_{n}
, we can replace the left-hand side above with absolute values. It therefore suffices to bound the right-hand side of (
C.4
) above.

Towards this direction, note that by
[
25
]
,
[
64
, Theorem 1]
, there exists
\delta_{0}
such that
\mathscr{H}_{[\ ]}(\delta,\mathcal{F}_{R},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty})\leq C\delta^{-\frac{d}{2}}
for all
\delta\leq\delta_{0}
, and some
C
depending on
R
. We can now invoke
3.1
with
\alpha=d/2
, for
d\geq 4
, to get:

\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{R}}\bigg|\int f\,d(\hat{\mu}_{n}-\mu)\bigg|\lesssim\begin{cases}n^{-\frac{2}{d}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta>\frac{2}{d-2}\\
n^{-\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta<\frac{2}{d-2}\end{cases}.

Here we have used the fact that
d/2>(1+\beta)/\beta
is equivalent to
\beta>2/(d-2)
for
d\geq 4
. We can bound the second term in the right hand side of (
C.4
) in the same way and recover the same bound as in the above display. This completes the proof of (
6.7
).
∎

Proof of
Theorem

6.4
, part (II).

Recall from part (I) that we can assume without loss of generality
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are supported in the ball of diameter
R
. By rescaling, if necessary, let us assume
R=1
. The proof of this result is motivated from
[
30
, Proposition 4]
and
[
62
, Theorem 3.3]
. We will of course need to use the maximal inequalities for dependent observations developed in our paper, in particular
3.1
.

We begin the proof with some notation. We write

{\sf exp}_{\varepsilon}:={\sf exp}\left(\frac{\cdot}{(\varepsilon/2)}\right).

For
\tilde{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
, define the set

L_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\mu}):=\{\phi:\mathcal{X}\to[-\infty,\infty):\ \phi\ \mbox{is measurable with}\ 0<\int{\sf exp}_{\varepsilon}(\phi)\,d\tilde{\mu}<\infty\}.

Given any
\phi\in L_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\mu})
, we define

\phi^{(\varepsilon,\tilde{\mu})}(y):=-\varepsilon\log\int{\sf exp}_{\varepsilon}(\phi(x)-\lVert x-y\rVert^{2})\,d\tilde{\mu}(x),\qquad y\in\mathcal{Y}.

Given
\tilde{\nu}\in\mathcal{Y}
, we define
L_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\nu})
and
\psi^{(\varepsilon,\tilde{\nu})}
for
\psi\in L_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\nu})
analogously. We further need two function classes, namely

\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}:=\cup_{\xi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})}\{\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}\ \mbox{such that}\ \exists\ \psi:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}\ \mbox{with}\ \phi=\psi^{(\varepsilon,\xi)},\ \lVert\phi\rVert_{\infty}\vee\lVert\psi\rVert_{\infty}\leq 3/2\},

and

\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon,\tilde{\mu}}:=\{\phi^{\varepsilon,\tilde{\mu}}:\ \phi\in\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\},\qquad\tilde{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}).

Recall the definition of the entropic optimal transport cost
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}
and the associated Sinkhorn divergence
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}
from (
6.5
) and (
6.6
) respectively. Now, by
[
110
, Proposition 2]
(also see
[
62
, Lemma 2.4]
), we have:

\mathbb{E}\big|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\mu,\nu)\big|\leq 2\mathbb{E}\bigg|\sup_{\phi\in\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}}\int\phi\,d(\hat{\mu}_{n}-\mu)\bigg|+2\mathbb{E}\bigg|\sup_{\psi\in\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}^{(\varepsilon,\hat{\mu}_{n})}}\int\psi\,d(\hat{\nu}_{n}-\nu)\bigg|.

By
[
62
, Theorem 3.3]
,
\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}^{(\varepsilon,\hat{\mu}_{n})}\subseteq\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}
, where

\mathfrak{\mathcal{X}}:=\{\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R},\ \lVert\phi\rVert_{\infty}\leq 3/2,\ \phi\ \mbox{is}\ 1-\mbox{Lipschitz and}\ 1-\mbox{concave}\}.

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is defined analogously. Consequently,

\mathbb{E}\big|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\mu,\nu)\big|\leq 2\mathbb{E}\bigg|\sup_{\phi\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X}}}\int\phi\,d(\hat{\mu}_{n}-\mu)\bigg|+2\mathbb{E}\bigg|\sup_{\psi\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\int\psi\,d(\hat{\nu}_{n}-\nu)\bigg|.

(C.19)

Importantly, the bound in the right hand side of (
C.19
) is free of
\varepsilon
. We now bound the first term on the right hand side above and the second term can be bounded similarly. By
[
25
, Theorem 6]
and
[
64
, Theorem 3.2]
, there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that we have:

\mathscr{H}_{[\ ]}(\delta,\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty})\leq C\delta^{-d/2},

for
\delta\leq\delta_{0}
, and
C
only depends on
\mathcal{X}
. By
3.1
with
\alpha=d/2
and (
C.19
), we then have for
d\geq 4
:

\mathbb{E}\big|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\mu,\nu)\big|\lesssim\begin{cases}\ \ n^{-\frac{2}{d}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta>\frac{2}{d-2}\\
n^{-\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta<\frac{2}{d-2}\end{cases}.

We can repeat the same argument to get

\mathbb{E}\big|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\mu}_{n})-\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\mu,\mu)\big|+\mathbb{E}\big|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\hat{\nu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,2}(\nu,\nu)\big|\lesssim\begin{cases}\ \ n^{-\frac{2}{d}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta>\frac{2}{d-2}\\
n^{-\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta<\frac{2}{d-2}\end{cases}.

Combining the above observations with the definition of the Sinkhorn divergence in (
6.6
), we get:

\mathbb{E}\bigg|\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon,2}(\mu,\nu)\bigg|\lesssim\begin{cases}\ \ n^{-\frac{2}{d}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta>\frac{2}{d-2}\\
n^{-\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta<\frac{2}{d-2}\end{cases}.

Finally by choosing
\varepsilon\equiv\varepsilon_{n}
as in the statement of
Theorem

6.4
,
we have by
C.6
,

\mathbb{E}\bigg|\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon_{n}(\beta,d),2}(\mu,\nu)-W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\bigg|\lesssim\begin{cases}\ \ n^{-\frac{2}{d}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta>\frac{2}{d-2}\\
n^{-\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta<\frac{2}{d-2}\end{cases}.

Finally by using the choice of
\varepsilon\equiv\varepsilon_{n}
and
k\equiv k_{n}
as in the statement of
Theorem

6.4
, coupled with
[
30
, Proposition 2]
, we get:

\mathbb{E}\bigg|\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon_{n},2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathcal{S}^{(k_{n})}_{\varepsilon_{n},2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})\bigg|\lesssim\begin{cases}\ \ n^{-\frac{2}{d}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta>\frac{2}{d-2}\\
n^{-\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}}&\mbox{if}\ \beta<\frac{2}{d-2}\end{cases}.

Combining the three displays above establishes (
6.8
).

Finally, as each step of the Sinkhorn algorithm (as described in
Section

6.4
) has
\tilde{O}(n^{2})
complexity, the total time complexity for obtaining
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon_{n},2}^{(k_{n})}
is
\tilde{O}(n^{2}k_{n})
. This completes the proof of
Theorem

6.4
, part (II).
∎

C.5
Proof of
Theorem

6.5

The proof proceeds along the same lines as
[
90
, Theorem 7.1]
and
[
66
, Theorem 3.5]
. Choose
r_{n}^{2}
such that
r_{n}^{2}n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1+\gamma^{-1}}}\to\infty
.

First, let us introduce some relevant notation. For
g\in\mathcal{G}
, write
f_{g}(x,y)=\mathbf{1}(y\neq g(x))
. Let
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}:=\{g\in\mathcal{G}:\ \mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G})\leq\delta\}
. Let
K
be the smallest integer such that
r_{n}^{2}2^{K}\geq 1
. For any
1\leq j\leq K
, let
\mathcal{F}_{j}:=\{f_{g_{1}}-f_{g_{2}}:\ g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{G}(r_{n}^{2}2^{j})\}
. Fix
M>0
and consider the event

E_{n}:=\left\{\max_{1\leq j\leq K}\frac{\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{j}}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)(f)|}{r_{n}^{2}2^{j}}\geq\frac{1}{4}\right\},

We will bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}(\hat{g}_{n})\geq r_{n}^{2})\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}(\hat{g}_{n})\geq r_{n}^{2},\ E_{n})+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}(\hat{g}_{n})\geq r_{n}^{2},\ E_{n}^{c}).

(C.20)

We will bound the two terms above one by one. For the first term of (
C.20
), note that by an application of Markov’s inequality, we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}(\hat{g}_{n})\geq r_{n}^{2},\ E_{n})\leq\mathbb{P}(E_{n})\leq 4\sum_{j=1}^{K}\frac{\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{j}}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)(f)|}{r_{n}^{2}2^{j}}.

Next we bound the expected supremum term above. To wit, recall that for every
g\in\mathcal{G}
, there exists
C\in\mathcal{C}
such that
g=\mathbf{1}(C)
. Next, let
\mathcal{S}:=\{S:f_{g}=\mathbf{1}(S)\}
. For
g_{1}=\mathbf{1}(C_{1})
and
g_{2}=\mathbf{1}(C_{2})
, we then have
f_{g_{1}}=\mathbf{1}(S_{1})
and
f_{g_{2}}=\mathbf{1}(S_{2})
for some
S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathcal{S}
. Therefore,

\mathbb{P}(S_{1}\Delta S_{2})=\mathbb{P}(f_{g-1}-f_{g_{2}})^{2}\leq\mathbb{P}(g_{1}-g_{2})^{2}=\mathbb{P}(C_{1}\Delta C_{2}).

Therefore,
\mathscr{H}_{[\,]}(u,\mathcal{S},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{1})\leq\mathscr{H}_{[\,]}(u,\mathcal{C},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{1})
for
0<u<1
. Further given any
g\in\mathcal{G}_{r_{n}^{2}2^{j}}
, let
S\in\mathcal{S}
be such that
f_{g}=\mathbf{1}(S)
. Recall that
g_{\star}(x)=\mathbf{1}(\mathbb{E}[Y|X=x]\geq 1/2)
. Then similar to the above display, we get:

\mathbb{P}(S\Delta S_{\star})\leq\mathbb{P}(g-g_{\star})^{2}\leq c^{-1}\mathcal{E}(g)\leq c^{-1}r_{n}^{2}2^{j}.

Note that we have used the margin condition (
6.10
) above. Therefore the following inequalities are immediate for
1\leq j\leq K
:

\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{j}}|\mathbb{G}_{n}(f)|\lesssim\mathbb{E}\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}(r_{n}^{2}2^{j})}|\mathbb{G}_{n}(f_{g}-f_{g_{\star}})|\lesssim\mathbb{E}\sup_{S\in\mathcal{S}:\ \mathbb{P}(S\Delta S_{\star})\leq c^{-1}r_{n}^{2}2^{j}}|\mathbb{G}_{n}(S\setminus S_{\star})|.

Now we use
Theorem

4.1
to get:

\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{j}}|\mathbb{G}_{n}(f)|\lesssim n^{\frac{\gamma(\alpha-1)+1}{2(\alpha\gamma+\gamma+1)}}+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+1}}(r_{n}^{2}2^{j})^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{1+\gamma}}+n^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}(r_{n}^{2}2^{j})^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2(1+\gamma)}}.

Combining everything, we get:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{K}\frac{\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{j}}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)(f)|}{r_{n}^{2}2^{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{r_{n}^{2}n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1+\gamma^{-1}}}}\sum_{j=1}^{K}2^{-j}\,+\frac{1}{(r_{n}^{2})^{1+\frac{\alpha\gamma}{1+\gamma}}n^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}}\sum_{j=1}^{K}(2^{-j})^{1+\frac{\alpha\gamma}{1+\gamma}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{r_{n}^{1+\frac{\alpha\gamma}{1+\gamma}}n^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}}\sum_{j=1}^{K}2^{-\frac{j}{2}\left(\frac{\alpha\gamma}{1+\gamma}-1\right)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{r_{n}^{2}n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1+\gamma^{-1}}}}+\frac{1}{(r_{n}^{2})^{1+\frac{\alpha\gamma}{1+\gamma}}n^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}}+\frac{1}{r_{n}^{1+\frac{\alpha\gamma}{1+\gamma}}n^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}},

where we have used
\alpha>1+\gamma^{-1}
in the last display. Finally as
r_{n}^{2}n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1+\gamma^{-1}}}\to\infty
, we also have
r_{n}^{1+\frac{\alpha\gamma}{1+\gamma}}n^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma+1)}}\to\infty
, and
r_{n}^{2+2\frac{\alpha\gamma}{1+\gamma}}n^{\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+1}}\to\infty
. As a result, we get:

\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}(\hat{g}_{n})\geq r_{n}^{2},\ E_{n})=0.

Now we move on to the second term of (
C.20
). We will show that on
E_{n}
, we have
\mathcal{E}(\hat{g}_{n})\leq r_{n}^{2}
almost surely. This will clearly complete the proof. Towards this direction, pick any
g\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\mathcal{E}(g)>r_{n}^{2}
. Then there exists
j\in[K]
such that
g\in\mathcal{G}_{r_{n}^{2}2^{j}}\setminus\mathcal{G}_{r_{n}^{2}2^{j-1}}
. Note that this implies the following important observation:

\mathcal{E}(g)\leq r_{n}^{2}2^{j}\leq 2\mathcal{E}(g).

(C.21)

Let us define

\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(g)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}(Y_{i}\neq g(X_{i}))\,-\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}(Y_{i}\neq\hat{g}_{n}(X_{i})(.

Now pick an arbitrary
u>0
such that
0<u<r_{n}^{2}2^{j}
and fix
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(u)
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(g)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(f_{g}-f_{g^{\prime}})+\mathbb{P}(f_{g^{\prime}}-f_{g_{\star}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(f_{g}-f_{g^{\prime}})+\mathcal{E}(g^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(f_{g}-f_{g^{\prime}})+u

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{n}(f_{g}-f_{g^{\prime}})+u+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{j}}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)(f)|

\displaystyle\leq\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(g)+u+\frac{1}{4}r_{n}^{2}2^{j}

\displaystyle\leq\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(g)+u+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(g).

As
u>0
can be arbitrarily small, we observe that on the event
E_{n}
, it holds that

\frac{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(g)}{\mathcal{E}(g)}\geq\frac{1}{2},

for all
g
such that
\mathcal{E}(g)>r_{n}^{2}
. As
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\hat{g}_{n})=0
, clearly
\hat{g}_{n}
does not satisfy the above inequality on
E_{n}
. Therefore we must have
\mathcal{E}(\hat{g}_{n})\leq r_{n}^{2}
on the even
E_{n}
. This completes the proof.

D
Proofs of supplementary results

Lemma D.1
.

Given a function class
\mathcal{F}
and a norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert
, suppose
\mathbb{H}_{\|\cdot\|}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
denotes the
\delta
-bracketing entropy of
\mathcal{F}
w.r.t. norm
\|\cdot\|
. Then, we have the following:

1.

For any monotone and
L
-Lipschitz function
\psi
, we have
\mathbb{H}_{\|\cdot\|}(\delta,\psi\circ\mathcal{F})\leq\mathbb{H}_{\|\cdot\|}(\delta/L,\mathcal{F})
.

2.

For any two function class
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathbb{H}_{\|\cdot\|}(\delta,\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{G})\leq\mathbb{H}_{\|\cdot\|}(\delta/2,\mathcal{F})+\mathbb{H}_{\|\cdot\|}(\delta/2,\mathcal{G})
.

3.

For any fixed function
g
, we have
\mathbb{H}_{\|\cdot\|}(\delta/\|g\|_{\infty},g\mathcal{F})\leq\mathbb{H}_{\|\cdot\|}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
.

4.

Let
f_{+}=f\vee 0
and
\mathcal{F}_{+}=\{f_{+}:\ f\in\mathcal{F}\}
. Then
\mathbb{H}_{\|\cdot\|}(\delta,\mathcal{F}_{+})\leq\mathbb{H}_{\|\cdot\|}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
.

Here
\psi\circ\mathcal{F}=\{\psi\circ f,f\in\mathcal{F}\}
,
g\mathcal{F}=\{gf:f\in\mathcal{F}\}
and
\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{G}=\{f+g:f\in\mathcal{F},g\in\mathcal{G}\}
.

Proof.

For the entire proof, first fix some
\delta>0
.

1.

Let
\psi
be a monotone Lipschitz function on the range of
\mathcal{F}
with Lipschitz constant
L
. Let
N=\mathbb{H}_{\left\|\cdot\right\|}(\delta/L,\mathcal{F})
with the brackets being
\left\{(l_{1},u_{1}),\dots,(l_{N},u_{N})\right\}
. By definition we have
\|u_{j}-l_{j}\|\leq\delta/L
for all
1\leq j\leq N
. Now given any
f
, there exists
1\leq j\leq N
such that
l_{j}(x)\leq f(x)\leq u_{j}(x)
for all
x
. Therefore, the monotonicity of
\phi
implies
\phi\circ l(x)\leq\phi\circ f(x)\leq\phi\circ u(x)
. Furthermore as
\phi
is
L
-Lipschitz, we have:

\|\phi\circ u-\phi\circ l\|\leq L\|u-l\|\leq L(\delta/L)=\delta.

Therefore the first claim is established.

2.

Let
\{(l_{1},u_{1}),\dots,(l_{N_{1}},u_{N_{1}})\}
be a
\delta/2
cover of
\mathcal{F}
and
\{(\tilde{l}_{1},\tilde{u}_{1}),\dots,(\tilde{l}_{N_{2}},\tilde{u}_{N_{2}})\}
be a
\delta/2
cover of
\mathcal{G}
. Then pick any function
f+g
from
\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{G}
. There exists
1\leq j_{1}\leq N_{1}
and
1\leq j_{2}\leq N_{2}
such that:

\displaystyle l_{j_{1}}(x)\leq f(x)\leq u_{j_{1}}(x),

\displaystyle\ \ \tilde{l}_{j_{2}}(x)\leq g(x)\leq\tilde{u}_{j_{2}}(x)

\displaystyle\implies l_{j_{1}}(x)+\tilde{l}_{j_{2}}(x)\leq f(x)+g(x)\leq u_{j_{1}}(x)+\tilde{u}_{j_{2}}(x)\,.

Furthermore, we have:

\|u_{j_{1}}+\tilde{u}_{j_{2}}-l_{j_{1}}-\tilde{l}_{j_{2}}\|\leq\|u_{j_{1}}-l_{j_{1}}\|+\|\tilde{u}_{j_{2}}-\tilde{l}_{j_{2}}\|\leq\delta/2+\delta/2\leq\delta\,.

This completes the proof.

3.

The proof is a combination of proof of (1.) and (2.). Any fixed function
g
can be written as
g_{+}-g_{-}
where both
g_{+}
and
g_{-}
are non-negative. In particular
g_{+}(x)=\max\{g(x),0\}
and
g_{-}(x)=\max\{-g(x),0\}
. As before, let
\{(l_{1},u_{1}),\dots,(l_{N_{1}},u_{N_{1}})\}
be a
\delta/2
cover of
\mathcal{F}
. Now for any
f\in\mathcal{F}
, there exists
1\leq j\leq N
such that
l(x)\leq f(x)\leq u(x)
for all
x
almost surely. Therefore, we have:

l(x)g_{+}(x)\leq g_{+}(x)f(x)\leq u(x)g_{+}(x),\ \ \text{ and }\ \ l(x)g_{-}(x)\leq g_{-}(x)f(x)\leq u(x)g_{-}(x)\,,

for all
x
. Therefore:

l(x)g_{+}(x)-u(x)g_{-}(x)\leq g(x)f(x)\leq u(x)g_{+}(x)-l(x)g_{-}(x)\,.

Furthermore:

\displaystyle\left\|u(x)g_{+}(x)-l(x)g_{-}(x)-l(x)g_{+}(x)+u(x)g_{-}(x)\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|(u(x)-l(x))g(x)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\|g\|_{\infty}\frac{\delta}{\|g\|_{\infty}}\leq\delta\,.

This completes the proof.

4.

Given
\delta>0
, let
[l_{1},u_{1}],\ldots,[l_{N},u_{N}]
denote the
\delta
-bracketing set of
\mathcal{F}
where
N={\sf exp}(\mathbb{H}_{\|\cdot\|}(\delta,\mathcal{F}))
. It suffices to show that
[l_{1+},u_{1+}],\ldots,[l_{N+},u_{N+}]
is a
\delta
-bracket of
\mathcal{F}_{+}
. To see this, given an
f\in\mathcal{F}
, let
1\leq i_{f}\leq N
be such that
l_{i_{f}}\leq f\leq u_{i_{f}}
and
\lVert u_{i_{f}}-l_{i_{f}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\delta
. Then
l_{i_{f}+}\leq f_{+}\leq u_{i_{f}+}
and
\lVert u_{i_{f}+}-l_{i_{f}+}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert u_{i_{f}}-l_{i_{f}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\delta
. This completes the proof.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

2.1
.

Observe that the above function is well-defined as the function

\beta_{q}-\frac{q}{n}\left(1+\sum_{k\geq 0\ :\ 2^{-k}\sigma\geq\delta}\mathbb{H}_{\phi}(2^{-k}\sigma,\mathcal{F})\right)

is
>0
at
q=0
and
\leq 0
for
q=n
. This also implies
\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\delta)\geq 1
. Further note that if
\delta_{1}\leq\delta_{2}
, then

\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({\delta_{1}},\mathcal{F})\geq\mathbb{H}_{\phi}({\delta_{2}},\mathcal{F})
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\beta(\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\delta_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\delta_{2})}{n}\left(1+\sum_{k\geq 0\ :\ 2^{-k}\sigma\geq\delta_{2}}\mathbb{H}_{\phi}(2^{-k}\sigma,\mathcal{F})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\delta_{1})}{n}\left(1+\sum_{k\geq 0\ :\ 2^{-k}\sigma\geq\delta_{2}}\mathbb{H}_{\phi}(2^{-k}\sigma,\mathcal{F})\right).

Therefore
\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\delta_{1})\leq\tilde{q}_{n,\beta}(\delta_{2})
. As
\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(q)
is non-decreasing in
q
, the conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

A.1
.

Choose any arbitrary
t>\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}
and sample
X\sim P
. By using the inequality

\phi^{c}(1)+\phi\left(\frac{h^{2}(x)}{t^{2}}\right)\geq\frac{h^{2}(x)}{t^{2}},

we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}h^{2}(X)\leq t^{2}\left(\phi^{c}(1)+1\right).

As
\phi\in\Phi
, we have
\phi^{c}(1)\in[0,\infty)
. Consequently, setting
c_{\phi}:=\sqrt{1+\phi^{c}(1)}
and taking infimum over
t\geq\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}
completes the proof.
∎

Proof.

We will use the covariance inequality from
[
124
, Theorem 1.1]
which implies that

|{\sf cov}(h(X_{i}),h(X_{j}))|\leq\int_{0}^{\beta(|i-j|)}Q_{h}^{2}(u)\,du.

Note that
Q_{h}(U)
,
U\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,1]
has the same distribution as
|h(X_{1})|
. Therefore, by
Lemma

A.1
, the right hand side of the above display is finite. Also note that by
Lemma

A.1
, it follows that:

\sum_{i=1}^{q}{\sf var}(h(X_{i}))\leq qc_{\phi}^{2}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}^{2}.

Combining the two displays above, we get:

\displaystyle{\sf var}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{q}h(X_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}{\sf var}(h(X_{i}))+2\sum_{i<j}{\sf cov}(h(X_{i}),h(X_{j}))

\displaystyle\leq qc_{\phi}^{2}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}^{2}+2\sum_{i<j}\int_{0}^{\beta(|i-j|)}Q_{h}^{2}(u)\,du

\displaystyle=qc_{\phi}^{2}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}^{2}+2\sum_{i<j}\int_{0}^{1}\mathds{1}\left(u\leq\beta(|i-j|)\right)Q_{h}^{2}(u)\,du

\displaystyle\leq qc_{\phi}^{2}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}^{2}+2q\sum_{k=1}^{q-1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathds{1}(u\leq\beta(k))Q_{h}^{2}(u)\,du.

(D.1)

Note that by Markov’s inequality, given any
s>0
and any
t>\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}
, we have:

\displaystyle P(|h(X)|\geq s)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\phi\left(\frac{h^{2}(X)}{t^{2}}\right)}{\phi\left(\frac{s^{2}}{t^{2}}\right)}\leq\frac{1}{\phi\left(\frac{s^{2}}{t^{2}}\right)}.

Next, given any
u>0
, we choose

s=t\sqrt{\phi^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)}

in which case,

P(|h(X)|\geq s)\leq u\implies Q_{h}(u)\leq t\sqrt{\phi^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)}.

Now by taking infimum over
t>\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}
, we get:

Q_{h}^{2}(u)\leq\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}^{2}\phi^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{u}\right).

(D.2)

The above observation implies

\sum_{k=1}^{q-1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathds{1}(u\leq\beta(k))Q_{h}^{2}(u)\,du\leq\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{q-1}\int_{0}^{\beta(k)}\phi^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)\,du\leq\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\phi,P}^{2}\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(q).

Plugging the above observation into (
D
) completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
A.1
.

In order to prove
A.1
, we will need two existing results from the literature which we state here without proof. The first result is a variant of Berbee’s coupling Lemma, which was proved independently in
[
8
]
and
[
59
]
.

Proposition D.1
.

Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
be a countable sequence of random elements taking values in some Polish space
\mathcal{X}
. For any
j\geq 1
, let
b_{j}:=\beta(X_{j},\ \{X_{i}\}_{i>j})
(see
Definition

2.1
). Then there exists a sequence
\{X_{i}^{*}\}_{i\geq 1}
of independent random variables such that for any
j\geq 1
,
X_{j}^{*}
has the same distribution as
X_{j}
and
P(X_{j}\neq X_{j}^{*})\leq b_{j}
.

The next result is the celebrated Bernstein’s inequality (see
[
9
]
).

Proposition D.2
.

Suppose
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
are centered independent random variables with
|Y_{i}|\leq b
and
{\sf var}(Y_{i})\leq\sigma^{2}_{Y}
. Then we have:

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\right|\geq t\right)\leq 2{\sf exp}{\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2\left(n\sigma^{2}_{Y}+\frac{1}{3}bt\right)}\right)}

for all
t>0
.

We are now in position to prove
A.1
.

Fix
q>0
. By Berbee’s coupling
D.1
, we can construct another sequence
\{X_{i}^{0}\}_{i\geq 1}
of random elements in
\mathcal{X}
such that:

1.

The sub-sequence
\mathbf{X}^{0}_{k}:=\{X^{0}_{qk+1},\dots,X^{0}_{q(k+1)}\}\overset{\mathscr{L}}{=}\{X_{qk+1},\dots,X_{q(k+1)}\}=:\mathbf{X}_{k}
.

2.

The sequence
\{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{2k}\}_{k\geq 1}
(respectively
\{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{2k-1}\}_{k\geq 1}
) are independent.

3.

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{X}^{0}_{k}\neq\mathbf{X}_{k}\right)\leq\beta_{q}
.

For the rest of the calculation, assume without loss of generality
Pf=0
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
. At the expense of potentially changing the universal constant
K
, we can assume without loss of generality that
n
is an even multiple of
q
. For
k\leq(n/q)-1
, define:

1.

Y_{k}(f):=\sum\limits_{i=q(k-1)+1}^{qk}f(X_{i})

2.

Y^{0}_{k}(f)=\sum\limits_{i=q(k-1)+1}^{qk}f(X^{0}_{i})

A simple application of triangle inequality yields:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i})\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X^{0}_{i})\right|\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(f(X_{i})-f(X^{0}_{i})\right)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X^{0}_{i})\right|\right]+\frac{2b}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}_{X_{i}\neq X_{i}^{0}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X^{0}_{i})\right|\right]+2b\sqrt{n}\beta_{q}\,.

(D.3)

We next bound the first term of the above inequality.

From Lemma
A.2
we have:

{\sf var}\left(\frac{Y_{k}^{0}}{\sqrt{q}}\right)={\sf var}\left(\frac{Y_{k}}{\sqrt{q}}\right)\leq\sigma^{2}\left(c_{\phi}^{2}+2(1+\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(q))\right)=\sigma^{2}\pi^{2}_{\phi,\beta}(q):=2\tau_{q}^{2}\,,

(D.4)

where
\pi_{\phi,\beta}(q)
is defined in (
A.1
). Further

\max_{k\geq 1}\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|Y_{k}(f)|\vee|Y_{k}^{0}(f)|\leq qb\,.

Therefore, for any
f\in\mathcal{F}
we have by the Bernstein’s inequality
D.2
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n/2q}Y^{0}_{2k}\right|>t\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2{\sf exp}{\left(-\frac{1}{2}\frac{nt^{2}}{\frac{n}{2q}2q\tau_{q}^{2}+\frac{1}{3}qb\sqrt{n}t}\right)}

\displaystyle=2{\sf exp}{\left(-\frac{1}{2}\frac{t^{2}}{\tau_{q}^{2}+\frac{1}{3}\frac{qb}{\sqrt{n}}t}\right)}

\displaystyle=2{\sf exp}{\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2\tau_{q}^{2}+\frac{2}{3}\frac{qb}{\sqrt{n}}t}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 2{\sf exp}{\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2\max\left\{2\tau_{q}^{2},\frac{2}{3}\frac{qb}{\sqrt{n}}t\right\}}\right)}

\displaystyle=2{\sf exp}{\left(-\min\left\{\frac{t^{2}}{4\tau_{q}^{2}},\frac{3t\sqrt{n}}{4qb}\right\}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 2{\sf exp}{\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{4\tau_{q}^{2}}\right)}+2{\sf exp}{\left(-\frac{3t\sqrt{n}}{4qb}\right)}

Therefore, taking union bound, we have:

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n/2q}Y^{0}_{2k}\right|>t\right)\leq 2{\sf exp}{\left(-\left[\frac{t^{2}}{4\tau_{q}^{2}}-\log{|\mathcal{F}|}\right]_{+}\right)}+2{\sf exp}{\left(-\left[\frac{3t\sqrt{n}}{4qb}-\log{|\mathcal{F}|}\right]_{+}\right)}

Same calculation will go through for the odd sequence
\{Y^{0}_{2k-1}\}
. Therefore we have:

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n/q}Y^{0}_{k}\right|>t\right)\leq 4{\sf exp}{\left(-\left[\frac{t^{2}}{16\tau_{q}^{2}}-\log{|\mathcal{F}|}\right]_{+}\right)}+4{\sf exp}{\left(-\left[\frac{3t\sqrt{n}}{8qb}-\log{|\mathcal{F}|}\right]_{+}\right)}

Integrating with respect to
t
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n/q}Y^{0}_{k}\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n/q}Y^{0}_{k}\right|>t\right)\ dt

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{0}^{\infty}{\sf exp}{\left(-\left[\frac{t^{2}}{16\tau_{q}^{2}}-\log{|\mathcal{F}|}\right]_{+}\right)}\ dt+4\int_{0}^{\infty}{\sf exp}{\left(-\left[\frac{3t\sqrt{n}}{8qb}-\log{|\mathcal{F}|}\right]_{+}\right)}\ dt

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{0}^{4\tau_{q}\sqrt{\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}}{\sf exp}{\left(-\left[\frac{t^{2}}{16\tau_{q}^{2}}-\log{|\mathcal{F}|}\right]_{+}\right)}\ dt+4\int_{4\tau_{q}\sqrt{\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}}^{\infty}{\sf exp}{\left(-\left[\frac{t^{2}}{16\tau_{q}^{2}}-\log{|\mathcal{F}|}\right]_{+}\right)}\ dt

\displaystyle\qquad+4\int_{0}^{\frac{8qb\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}{3\sqrt{n}}}{\sf exp}{\left(-\left[\frac{3t\sqrt{n}}{8qb}-\log{|\mathcal{F}|}\right]_{+}\right)}\ dt+4\int_{\frac{8qb\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}{3\sqrt{n}}}^{\infty}{\sf exp}{\left(-\left[\frac{3t\sqrt{n}}{8qb}-\log{|\mathcal{F}|}\right]_{+}\right)}\ dt

\displaystyle\leq 16\tau_{q}\sqrt{\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}+4|\mathcal{F}|\int_{4\tau_{q}\sqrt{\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}}^{\infty}{\sf exp}{\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{16\tau_{q}^{2}}\right)}\ dt+\frac{32qb\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}{3\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\qquad+4|\mathcal{F}|\int_{\frac{8qb\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}{3\sqrt{n}}}^{\infty}{\sf exp}{\left(-\frac{3t\sqrt{n}}{8qb}\right)}\ dt

\displaystyle\leq 16\tau_{q}\sqrt{\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}+16\tau_{q}+\frac{32qb\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}{3\sqrt{n}}+\frac{32qb}{3\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle=32\tau_{q}\sqrt{1+\log{|\mathcal{F}|}}+\frac{32qb}{3\sqrt{n}}\left(1+\log{|\mathcal{F}|}\right)\,.

This along with (
D.3
) completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

A.3
.

We begin by noting that for
t\leq\varepsilon
, we have:

\displaystyle Q_{h}(t)\leq\frac{\omega_{q,h}(\varepsilon)}{\sqrt{B_{q}(t)}}.

(D.5)

As
Q_{h}^{-1}(Q_{h}(\varepsilon))\leq\varepsilon
, we consequently get,

\displaystyle\lVert h\mathds{1}(|h|>Q_{h}(\varepsilon))\rVert_{L_{1}(P)}=\int_{0}^{Q_{h}^{-1}(Q_{h}(\varepsilon))}Q_{h}(t)\,dt\leq\omega_{q,h}(\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\sqrt{B_{q}(t)}}\,dt.

The last inequality above follows from (
D.5
).
Next we observe that
B_{q}
is an integral of a bounded decreasing function and hence, is concave, with
B_{q}(0)=0
. Hence we have:

B_{q}(t)=B_{q}\left(\varepsilon\frac{t}{\varepsilon}+\left(1-\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right)0\right)\geq\frac{t}{\varepsilon}B_{q}(\varepsilon)\,.

Using this we obtain:

\lVert h\mathds{1}(|h|>Q_{h}(\varepsilon))\rVert_{L_{1}(P)}\leq\frac{\omega_{q,h}(\varepsilon)\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{B_{q}(\varepsilon)}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\frac{\,dt}{\sqrt{t}}=\frac{2\omega_{q,h}(\varepsilon)\varepsilon}{\sqrt{B_{q}(\varepsilon)}}.

This proves (
A.4
). The proof of (
A.6
) follows from (
A.4
) and the following observations. Note that as
Q_{h}
is decreasing:

\displaystyle\omega_{q,h}^{2}(\varepsilon)\leq\sup_{t\leq\varepsilon}\ Q_{h}^{2}(t)B_{q}(t)\leq\sup_{t\leq\varepsilon}\ \int_{0}^{t}Q_{h}^{2}(u)\left(\sum_{k=0}^{q}\mathds{1}(u\leq\beta(k))\right)\,du.

By invoking (
D.2
), we then have for all
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
:

\displaystyle\omega_{q,h}(\varepsilon)\leq\lVert h\rVert_{\phi,P}\sqrt{\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(q)}.

(D.6)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle B_{q}(\varepsilon)\geq\varepsilon\sum_{k=0}^{q}\mathds{1}(\varepsilon\leq\beta(k)).

(D.7)

Using (
D.7
) and (
A.5
), we then get:

v\sqrt{B_{q}(\varepsilon)}\geq\upsilon\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{q}\mathds{1}(\varepsilon\leq\beta(k))}\geq\lVert h\rVert_{\phi,P}\sqrt{\Lambda_{\phi,\beta}(q)}\geq\omega_{q,h}(\varepsilon)\geq Q_{h}(\varepsilon)\sqrt{B_{q}(\varepsilon)}\,.

Here the first inequality follows from (
D.7
), second inequality from the condition (
A.5
), third inequality from (
D.6
) and the last one from the definition of
\omega_{q,h}
. Consequently, for any
v\geq Q_{h}(\varepsilon)
we have:

\lVert h\mathds{1}(|h|>v)\rVert_{L_{1}(P)}\leq\lVert h\mathds{1}(|h|>Q_{h}(\varepsilon))\rVert_{L_{1}(P)}\,.

The conclusion then follows by combining (
D.6
), (
D.7
), and (
A.4
).
∎

Proof of
A.2
.

The proof is similar to that of
A.1
except that, instead of using
Lemma

A.2
in (
D.4
), we will use the following:

\displaystyle{\sf var}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{q}h(X_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}{\sf var}(h(X_{i}))+2\sum_{i<j}{\sf cov}(h(X_{i}),h(X_{j}))

\displaystyle\leq q\lVert h\rVert_{\infty,\mathcal{X}}^{2}+2\sum_{i<j}\int_{0}^{\beta(|i-j|)}Q_{h}^{2}(u)\,du

\displaystyle\leq q\lVert h\rVert_{\infty,\mathcal{X}}^{2}+2q\lVert h\rVert_{\infty,\mathcal{X}}^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{q-1}\beta_{k}.

(D.8)

Here the second inequality follows from
[
124
, Theorem 1.1]
and the third inequality follows from the fact that
\lVert Q_{h}\rVert_{\infty,(0,1)}\leq\lVert h\rVert_{\infty,\mathcal{X}}
.
The rest of the proof follows verbatim from the proof of
A.1
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

A.4
.

The proof is is the same as that of
Lemma

A.3
except that we will replace (
D.6
) with the following bound:

\omega_{q,h}(\varepsilon)\leq\sup_{t\leq\varepsilon}\sqrt{Q_{h}^{2}(t)B_{q}(t)}\leq\lVert h\rVert_{\infty,\mathcal{X}}\sqrt{B_{q}(1)}\leq\lVert h\rVert_{\infty,\mathcal{X}}\sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{q}\beta_{k}},

for all
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
. The rest of the proof follows verbatim from the proof of
Lemma

A.3
.
∎

Proof of
A.3
.

The proof is similar to that of
A.1
except that, instead of using
Lemma

A.2
in (
D.4
), we will use the following:

\displaystyle{\sf var}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{q}h(X_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}{\sf var}(h(X_{i}))+2\sum_{i<j}{\sf cov}(h(X_{i}),h(X_{j}))

\displaystyle\leq q\lVert h\rVert_{L_{2}(P)}^{2}+2\lVert h\rVert_{L_{2}(P)}^{2}\sum_{i<j}\gamma_{|i-j|}

\displaystyle\leq qb^{2-\tilde{r}}\sigma^{\tilde{r}}+2qb^{2-\tilde{r}}\sigma^{\tilde{r}}\sum_{k=1}^{q-1}\gamma_{k}.

Here the second inequality follows from the definition of
\rho
-mixing (see
Definition

4.1
) and the third inequality follows from the elementary inequality
\int h^{2}\,dP\leq\lVert h\rVert_{\infty,\mathcal{X}}^{2-\tilde{r}}\int|h|^{\tilde{r}}\,dP
.
The rest of the proof follows verbatim from the proof of
A.1
.
∎
